Appendix B
Hamiltonian Lattice Gauge Theory on General Graphs

Let us now delve into the extension of the framework of lattice Hamiltonian Yang-Mills theory, as developed by Kogut and Susskind (KS)
[
4
]
, to general graphs.

B.1
Graph theory

We define a graph
\mathrm{G}
through
[
94
]
a finite set of vertices
\Gamma
and a set of half-links (also known as half-
e
dges in the mathematical literature)
H
, equipped with an anchoring map
\pi:H\rightarrow\Gamma
and an attaching map
\sigma:H\rightarrow H
.

Fig.

20
shows a few examples that illustrate the definitions used.
The anchoring map anchors each half-link to its corresponding vertex.
We will demand that it be surjective, meaning that there are no vertices without half-links attached to it.
The attachment map attaches each half-link to its partner, with which it will form a full link.
Accordingly, we will ask that for each
h\in H
we have
\sigma(\sigma(h))=h
, i.e.
\sigma
is an involution.
Also, in the theory of Feynman diagrams one is often interested in graphs with external links.
Such links can be characterized by half-links
h\in H
which are their own partner
\sigma(h)=h
.
For our purposes we will not allow this possibility.

We will define an undirected link to be a set of the form
\{h,\sigma(h)\}\subseteq H
.
We will denote the set of all undirected links by
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.09999pt}{$\displaystyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.58333pt}{$\displaystyle{\sigma}$}}{\raisebox{4.09999pt}{$\textstyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.58333pt}{$\textstyle{\sigma}$}}{\raisebox{2.87pt}{$\scriptstyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.80832pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sigma}$}}{\raisebox{2.05pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sigma}$}}}
.
By a directed link, we will refer to an ordering of such an orbit
e=(h,\sigma(h))\in H\times H
, representing the link going from
\sigma(h)
to
h
.
Throughout this paper, whenever we refer to a link without any additional qualification, the reader should assume we are referring to a directed link.
The set of all links will be denoted
H\times_{\sigma}H
, where the notation used highlights the fact that the set of links is simply the graph of
\sigma
.
This set is equipped with maps that invert the direction of the link

\displaystyle H\times_{\sigma}H\rightarrow H\times_{\sigma}H\,,\hskip 23.49976pte=(h_{1},h_{2})\mapsto e^{-1}\coloneq(h_{2},h_{1})\,,

(119)

determine its source

\displaystyle s:H\times_{\sigma}H\rightarrow\Gamma\,,\hskip 23.49976pte=(h_{1},h_{2})\mapsto s(e)=\pi(h_{2})\,,

(120)

and determine its target

\displaystyle t:H\times_{\sigma}H\rightarrow\Gamma\,,\hskip 23.49976pte=(h_{1},h_{2})\mapsto t(e)=\pi(h_{1})\,.

(121)

Finally, a corner is a pair of half-links
(h_{1},h_{2})\in H
that are anchored at the same vertex
\pi(h_{1})=\pi(h_{2})
. Let us denote the set of corners by
C
.

(a)

(b)

(c)

Figure 20:
On the left there is the graph determined by
\Gamma=\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
,
H=\{h_{1},h_{2}\}
,
\pi(h_{1})=p_{1}
,
\pi(h_{2})=p_{2}
, and
\sigma(h_{1})=h_{2}
.
On the middle we have a graph determined by taking
p_{1}=p_{2}=p
.
These are the only two graphs with a single undirected link.
On the right we have the graph
\Gamma=\{p,q,r\}
,
H=\{a,b,c,d,e,f,g,h\}
,
\pi(a)=\pi(b)=\pi(d)=\pi(f)=p
,
\pi(c)=\pi(g)=q
,
\pi(h)=\pi(e)=r
,
\sigma(a)=b
,
\sigma(c)=d
,
\sigma(e)=f
, and
\sigma(g)=h
.
The set of undirected links of the latter is
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.09999pt}{$\displaystyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.58333pt}{$\displaystyle{\sigma}$}}{\raisebox{4.09999pt}{$\textstyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.58333pt}{$\textstyle{\sigma}$}}{\raisebox{2.87pt}{$\scriptstyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.80832pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sigma}$}}{\raisebox{2.05pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sigma}$}}}=\{\{a,b\},\{c,d\},\{e,f\},\{g,h\}\}
.
The set of links on the other hand is
H\times_{\sigma}H=\{(a,b),(b,a),(c,d),(d,c),(e,f),(f,e),(g,h),(h,g)\}
.
At the vertex
q
the set of corners is
\{(g,g),(c,c),(g,c),(c,g)\}
.
At the vertex
p
there are
4+(3!!\times 2)=10
corners.

The set of links naturally extends to the set of trajectories
H\times_{\sigma}H\subseteq T
(see
Fig.

21
for an example).
The set
T_{n}
of trajectories of length
n
is the set of maps
\gamma:\{1,\dots,n\}\rightarrow H\times_{\sigma}H
such that for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
we have
t(\gamma(i))=s(\gamma(i+1))
.
Each link can be identified with a trajectory of length 1, and we can therefore identify
H\times_{\sigma}H=T_{1}
. The union of all possible paths will be called
T

T=\bigsqcup_{n=1}^{\infty}T_{n}\,.

(122)

Indeed, the inversion, source and target maps extend to all of
T

\displaystyle T_{n}\rightarrow T_{n}\,,\hskip 23.49976pt

\displaystyle\gamma\mapsto\gamma^{-1}:i\mapsto\gamma^{-1}(i)\coloneq\gamma(n-i+1)^{-1},

(123)

\displaystyle s,t:T_{n}\rightarrow\Gamma\,,\hskip 23.49976pt

\displaystyle\gamma\mapsto s(\gamma)\coloneq s(\gamma(1))\mbox{\hskip 11.74988ptand\hskip 11.74988pt}t(\gamma)\coloneq t(\gamma(n))\,.

(124)

Whenever we have two trajectories
\gamma_{1}\in T_{n}
and
\gamma_{2}\in T_{m}
satisfying
t(\gamma_{1})=s(\gamma_{2})
, i. e. the starting point of the second path is equal to the end point of the first path, they can be concatenated to a new trajectory
\gamma_{2}\gamma_{1}\in T_{n+m}
that first goes through
\gamma_{1}
and then through
\gamma_{2}

\gamma_{2}\gamma_{1}(i)=\begin{cases}\gamma_{1}(i)&i\in\{1,\dots,n\}\\
\gamma_{2}(i-n)&i\in\{n+1,\dots,n+m\}\,,\end{cases}

(125)

We will further call a trajectory
\gamma\in T_{n}
closed if it starts and ends at the same point
s(\gamma(1))=t(\gamma(n))
.

Much like trajectories are continuous sequences of links, we can also define corner trajectories
\gamma:\{1,\dots,n\}\rightarrow C
which are continuous sequences of corners.
An example of this is also shown in
Fig.

21
.
Much like trajectories, corner trajectories can also be inverted, composed, and have source and target maps.
The first two are defined by the same equations as for trajectories.
The source and target are instead defined by the equations

\gamma(1)=(\sigma(s(\gamma)),s(\gamma))\mbox{\hskip 11.74988ptand\hskip 11.74988pt}\gamma(n)=(t(\gamma),\sigma(t(\gamma)))\,.

(126)

In particular, they are half-links instead of vertices.

Let us wrap up our graph-theoretic definitions by introducing the notion of an orientation.
This is obtained by choosing a unique orientation for each undirected link.
It is thus given by a set of links
E\subseteq H\times_{\sigma}H
such that, for all links
e\in H\times_{\sigma}H
, either
e\in E
or
e^{-1}\in E
, but not both.
In the literature most of the theory is usually explained by heavily emphasizing the role of
E
.
However, while choosing an
E
is useful for computations, the orientation does not have any real physical meaning – physical observables should be independent of
E
.
Accordingly, in order to have more conceptual clarity, throughout our discussion we will do our best to deemphasize the role of
E
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 21:
On the left we show the trajectory
\gamma\in T_{3}
of length 3 given by
\gamma(1)=(j,i)
,
\gamma(2)=(h,g)
, and
\gamma(3)=(f,e)
. We have
s(\gamma)=s
and
t(\gamma)=p
. The inverse trajectory would then be given by
\gamma^{-1}(1)=(e,f)
,
\gamma^{-1}(2)=(g,h)
, and
\gamma^{-1}(3)=(i,j)
. We also have a corner trajectory
\gamma_{c}
on the right given by
\gamma_{c}(1)=(g,j)
,
\gamma_{c}(2)=(e,h)
. It follows the same path as
\gamma
except that it doesn’t go through
i
or
f
.

B.2
Classical Yang-Mills theory

In order to specify a Yang-Mills theory we need to choose a structure group
G
(note the difference between this
G
and the
\mathrm{G}
we use to denote a graph).
We will restrict to compact semisimple Lie groups in this work.
The kinematical information of the theory is encoded in Wilson lines defined on the links of the lattice.
The classical field configurations
\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G})
consist of all maps
u:H\times_{\sigma}H\rightarrow G
assigning to each link the corresponding Wilson line supported on it.
Then, for every link
e\in H\times_{\sigma}H
, the group element
u(e)\in G
tells us how to perform parallel transport of the matter degrees of freedom from
s(e)
to
t(e)
.
Of course, the transport from
t(e)
to
s(e)
can be implemented by doing the opposite operation.
Thus, we will further restrict the maps in
\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G})
to satisfy

u(e^{-1})=u(e)^{-1}\,.

(127)

Note that a field configuration is completely specified by the configuration at each undirected link.
In other words, the space of field configurations is the Cartesian product of the space of field configurations on each undirected link.
Furthermore, the configuration of a field on such an undirected link is completely determined by the configuration of a field on one of its orientations.
We then conclude that

\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G})\cong G^{E}\,,

(128)

for any orientation
E
.
We remark againt that, although the choice of such an orientation is useful for computations, it is unphysical and thus we will try to not put emphasis on it for the time being.
Finally, let us also note that classical field configurations naturally assign Wilson lines to trajectories
\gamma\in T
as well

u(\gamma)\coloneq\overleftarrow{\prod_{\{e\in\gamma\}}}u(e)\coloneq u(\gamma(n))\cdots u(\gamma(1))\,.

(129)

Observe that the order chosen here reflects the physical interpretation of
u
as implementing parallel transport. First
u(\gamma(1))
acts on degrees of freedom at
s(\gamma(1))
and maps them to degrees of freedom at
t(\gamma(1))
.
Then
u(\gamma(2))
acts on degrees of freedom at
t(\gamma(1))=s(\gamma(2))
and maps them to degrees of freedom at
t(\gamma(2))
.

The fields in
\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G})
are however not all physically distinguishable from one another.
In order to describe Yang-Mills theory locally, as we will explain more below, the space of fields has an inherent redundancy.
This redundancy is parametrized by a group element at each vertex of the graph.
The set
G^{\Gamma}
of such assignments is called the gauge group.
This group acts on the field configurations in a way that we define two fields to be physically equivalent if one can be transformed into the other by a gauge transformation.

In order to describe this action, it is useful to, as an intermediate step, define an action of the structure group that is supported on the half-link
h\in H
.
It will be non-trivial only on the two Wilson lines with support at the half-link, acting differently depending on whether the line is coming into or out of the half-link.
Said group action can be succinctly summarized as
6
6

6

Note how the middle line in this expression is completely determined by the first line
(gu(e^{-1}))^{-1}=u(e)g^{-1}
.
From now on we will omit these redundancies from our expressions. The reader should however remain alert of these detail when reading “otherwise” from now on.

(g\triangleright_{h}u)(e)\coloneq\begin{cases}gu(e)&e=(h,\sigma(h)),\\
u(e)g^{-1}&e=(\sigma(h),h)\\
u(e)&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

(130)

A graphical interpretation of this action can be found in the left of
Fig.

22
.
The idea here is that, if
h
is at the end of the link
e
, then the transformation
g
acts after the parallel transport has been implemented.
If instead
h
is at the beginning, the transformation acts before the parallel transport takes place.
The appearance of
g^{-1}
in this case ensures that the transformed field configuration still satisfies the consistency condition
u(e)^{-1}=u(e^{-1})
.
The action of a full gauge transformation
g\in G^{\Gamma}
is obtained by letting the transformation
g(p)
at each vertex
p\in\Gamma
act on each of the half-links
h\in\pi^{-1}(\{p\})
attached to it.
In particular, since every link is attached to two vertices, we simply have

\displaystyle(g\triangleright u)(h_{1},h_{2})\coloneq

\displaystyle(g(\pi(h_{1}))\triangleright_{h_{1}}g(\pi(h_{2}))\triangleright_{h_{2}}u)(h_{1},h_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(\pi(h_{1}))u(h_{1},h_{2})g(\pi(h_{2}))^{-1}\,.

(131)

This action is depicted at the right of
Fig.

22
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 22:
On the left it is shown how gauge transformations act locally on every half-link of a graph.
On the right it is shown how full gauge transformations act on the Wilson line supported on
(f,e)
.
All of the other Wilson lines are similarly affected by the gauge transformation simulaneously.

B.3
Quantum Yang-Mills theory

The Haar measure provides a natural way of integrating over the structure group.
This in turn induces a natural integration measure on the space of field configurations
\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G})
.
Explicitly, given an orientation
E
, one can use the isomorphism (
128
) and set

\differential{u}=\prod_{e\in E}\differential{u(e)}\,.

(132)

The resulting measure is independent of
E
due to the consistency condition (
127
) and the invariance of the Haar measure under the inversion operation.
In particular we can use this measure to define densities of such field configurations.

In the quantum theory, the states of the theory are described by wave functions that specify the probability amplitude density for each classical field configuration.
The Hilbert space of the theory will therefore be

\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{G})\coloneq L^{2}\quantity(\fields)\,.

(133)

Given that one can specify the field configuration independenty at each undirected link, this space factorizes as the tensor product of the Hilbert spaces on each of the undirected links.
Indeed, through a choice of orientation
E
, (
128
) induces

\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{G})\cong\bigotimes_{e\in E}L^{2}(G)\,.

(134)

The Hilbert space is spanned by states
\ket{u}
in which the field is in a definite configuration
u\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G})
.
More general wave functions are then given by linear combinations

\ket{\psi}=\int_{\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G})}\differential{u}\ket{u}\psi(u),\hskip 11.74988pt\psi(u)=\innerproduct{u}{\psi}\,.

(135)

The state space
\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{G})
is equipped with a field operator
U
that measures the field configuration.
Each trajectory
\gamma\in T
supports a
G
-valued Wilson line operator

U(\gamma)\ket{u}=u(\gamma)\ket{u}\,.

(136)

Given that they are all simultaneously diagonalizable, the matrix elements of this operator all commute with each other (although the full operators in general don’t commute unless
G
is Abelian).
On the other hand, every half-link
h\in H
supports a (chromo)electric operator
7
7

7

These are sometimes called electric field operators in the literature given that their role in the Hamiltonian will be very similar to the role of the electric field in its continuum counterpart.
However, this identification can also lead to confusion.
For example, while in our case the electric operator will generate gauge transformations on its support, in the continuum the integral of the covariant derivative of the electric field plays this role.
.
They are defined through a representation of the group on the Hilbert space, induced naturally from the action of the group on the classical field configurations

R_{h}(g)\ket{u}\coloneq\ket{g\triangleright_{h}u}\,.

(137)

The electric operators
E_{X}(h)
are then the generators of this representation
8
8

8

In this paper we will follow the mathematicians convention that Lie algebras of unitary groups are anti-hermitian.
We however follow the physicists convention of including factors of
i
to obtain Hermitian operators from the representation of such Lie algebras.

R_{h}(e^{\omega X})=e^{-i\omega E_{X}(h)},\hskip 11.74988ptX\in\mathfrak{g}\coloneq\text{Lie}(G)\,.

(138)

In particular the electric operators bundle into a
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
-valued operator.
Explicitly, if
X_{1},\dots,X_{\dim G}
is a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
and
X^{1},\dots,X^{\dim G}
is a basis of the dual, we have

E(h)=X^{a}E_{X_{a}}(h)\equiv X^{a}E_{a}(h)\,.

(139)

Since the electric operators
-iE(h)
furnish a representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of
G
, they satisfy its commutation relations

[E_{X}(h),E_{Y}(h^{\prime})]=iE_{[X,Y]}(h)\delta_{h,h^{\prime}}\,.

(140)

The Wilson line operators transform like

R_{h}(g)^{-1}U(\gamma)R_{h}(g)=(g\triangleright_{h}U)(\gamma)\,,

(141)

where we have extended the group action on the classical field configurations to their quantum counterparts

(g\triangleright_{h}U)(\gamma)\ket{u}\coloneq(g\triangleright_{h}u)(\gamma)\ket{u}\,.

(142)

This induces an antirepresentation of the Lie algebra
9
9

9

Since the action on the Wilson line is from the right, it reverses the order of the product of elements in
G
.
This is why we obtain an anti-representation, with the crucial minus sign in (
143
)

\displaystyle[-iE_{X}(h),U(h_{1},h_{2})]=-XU(h_{1},h_{2})\delta_{h_{1},h}+U(h_{1},h_{2})X\delta_{h_{2},h}\,.

(143)

At the quantum level, gauge transformations act by a representation
R
of the gauge group
G^{\Gamma}
on
\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{G})

R(g)\ket{u}=\ket{g\triangleright u}\,.

(144)

The generator of these is given by the Gauss operator at each vertex
p\in\Gamma

G(p)=\sum_{h\in\pi^{-1}(\{p\})}E(h)\,.

(145)

The physical Hilbert space is then identified as the space of states
\mathfrak{h}(\mathrm{G})
that are gauge invariant, i.e. annihilated by the Gauss operators

\mathfrak{h}(\mathrm{G})=\bigcap_{p\in\Gamma}\ker G(p)\,.

(146)

These correspond to wave functions that are invariant under gauge transformations

\innerproduct{g\triangleright u}{\psi}=\matrixelement{u}{R(g^{-1})}{\psi}=\innerproduct{u}{\psi}\,.

(147)

(a)

(b)

Figure 23:
Examples of admissible spacetime lattices. They are distinguished by the fact that they have elementary plaquettes.
On the left we have a square lattice, whose elementary plaquettes are the smallest squares.
On the right picture we have a honeycomb lattice, whose elementary plaquettes are given by the smallest hexagons.

The Hamiltonian of the system depends on the choice of a coupling constant
g^{2}
, an invariant inner product
\expectationvalue{\cdot,\cdot}
on
\mathfrak{g}
, and the interpretation of the graph
\mathrm{G}
.
For the groups that we are interested in, we can take as the inner product

\expectationvalue{X,Y}=-2\tr(XY)\,,

(148)

with the trace computed in the fundamental representation
10
10

10

A different choice of inner product ammounts to a redefinition in the coupling constant.
.
On the other hand, for now, let us think of
\Gamma
as a graph superimposed in space.
Such spatial graphs are distinguished for having a set of closed loops
\mathcal{P}\subseteq T
known as plaquettes, such as the ones in
Fig.

23
.
Whenever there is a plaquette
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}
, the loop with the reversed orientation is also a plaquette
\gamma^{-1}\in\mathcal{P}
.
The magnetic part of the Hamiltonian is given by the sum of Wilson loops

H_{B}\coloneq\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{P}}W(\gamma),\hskip 11.74988ptW(\gamma)\coloneq\tr(U(\gamma))\,.

(149)

In particular it gives standard operator on
\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{G})
not a
G
-valued one.
On the other hand, the electric Hamiltonian is given by

H_{E}\coloneq\sum_{\{h_{1},h_{2}\}\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{2.87pt}{$\displaystyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.80832pt}{$\displaystyle{\sigma}$}}{\raisebox{2.87pt}{$\textstyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.80832pt}{$\textstyle{\sigma}$}}{\raisebox{2.009pt}{$\scriptstyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.26582pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sigma}$}}{\raisebox{1.435pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-0.90416pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sigma}$}}}}E^{2}(h)\,.

(150)

In here
E^{2}(h)
is the Casimir operator with respect to our inner product of the representation at either of the two half links
h\in\{h_{1},h_{2}\}
.
It is conveniently described in any basis as

E^{2}(h)\coloneq E_{a}(h)E^{a}(h)\,.

(151)

E_{a}
denotes the components of an operator
E_{a}X^{a}
in
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, and we can use the inner product to raise its indices and make it an operator in
\mathfrak{g}
.
We will explain in the next section why the choice of half-link
h\in\{h_{1},h_{2}\}
does not matter in the Hamiltonian.

The full Hamiltonian of the theory is given by (
1
)

H=\frac{g^{2}}{2}H_{E}+\frac{1}{2g^{2}}H_{B}\,.

(152)

This is controlled by a dimensionless coupling constant
g^{2}
.
For strong coupling
g^{2}\gg 1
, the theory is in the electric regime, where the behaviour is dominated by the electric Hamiltonian.
At weak coupling
g^{2}\ll 1
, the theory is in the magnetic regime.
Performing computations in a magnetic basis
[
52
]
is better suited in this regime.

B.4
SU(2) prepotentials on general graphs

B.4.1
Peter-Weyl theorem

We are now ready to introduce the localization of the degrees of freedom from links to half-links.
This is most easily done by first focusing on a single link in light of (
134
).
We will thus restrict to the graph shown on
Fig.

1a
.
Let us for the moment further orient the link
e=(h_{1},h_{2})
.
Then the field configurations can be identified with Wilson lines along said link and the Hilbert space
L^{2}(G)
is given by wave functions describing the probability amplitude densities for such a line.
In particular, this Hilbert space is a birepresentation of
G
according to its action on each of the half-links

\displaystyle R_{h_{1}}(g_{1})R_{h_{2}}(g_{2})\ket{u}=\ket{g_{1}ug_{2}^{-1}}\hskip 11.74988pt\Leftrightarrow\hskip 11.74988pt\matrixelement{u}{R_{h_1}(g_1)R_{h_2}(g_2)}{\psi}=\innerproduct{g_1^{-1}ug_2}{\psi}\,.

(153)

The technique we will use stems from the Peter-Weyl theorem

L^{2}(G)\cong\bigoplus_{r\in\hat{G}}(V_{r}\otimes V_{r}^{*})\,,

(154)

with
\hat{G}
an exhaustive collection of the inequivalent irreducible unitary representations of
G
,
V_{r}
the
r
representation and
V_{r}^{*}
its dual.
Let us understand this isomorphism in detail.
The isomorphism is given by interpreting an elementary operator
\outerproduct{\psi}{\phi}\in V_{r}\otimes V_{r}^{*}
in the
r
representation as a wave function given by
11
11

11

We remark that on a finite dimensional Hilbert space, such as
V_{r}
, every operator can be written in terms of its matrix elements, so that the space of operators is equal to
V_{r}\otimes V_{r}^{*}
.
However, a tensor product of two Hilbert spaces is, in itself, a Hilbert space.
Thus, the space of operators on
V_{r}
is, in itself, a Hilbert space.
In here we will be using that that Hilbert space can be identified as “the Hilbert space of chromoelectric flux
r
” in the Yang-Mills theory.

\bra{u}\quantity(\op{\psi}{\phi})\coloneq\sqrt{\dim V_{r}}\matrixelement{\phi}{r(u)}{\psi}\,.

(155)

The localization to half-links
is obtained because this is an equality of birepresentations

\displaystyle\bra{u}\quantity(r(g_2)\op{\psi}{\phi}r(g_1^{-1}))

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\dim V_{r}}\matrixelement{\phi}{r(g_1^{-1})r(u)r(g_2)}{\psi}

\displaystyle=\bra{g_{1}^{-1}ug_{2}}\quantity(\op{\psi}{\phi})

\displaystyle=\bra{u}R_{h_{2}}(g_{2})R_{h_{1}}(g_{1})\quantity(\op{\psi}{\phi})\,.

(156)

In particular, the first factor
V_{r}
contains the gauge redundancy of the starting point
h_{2}
while the second factor
V_{r}^{*}
contains the gauge redundancy of endpoint
h_{1}
.
This localization suggests an enlargement of the Hilbert space to

L^{2}(G)\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\text{SB}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\text{SB}}^{*},\hskip 11.74988pt\mathcal{H}_{\text{SB}}\coloneq\bigoplus_{r\in\hat{G}}V_{r}\,.

(157)

Each of the two
\mathcal{H}_{\text{SB}}
factors is thus supported on a single half-link.
The original Hilbert space is then obtained by the introduction of new
Abelian
Gauss’ laws demanding that physical vectors are spanned by tensor products of vectors in the same irreducible representation.

B.4.2
Schwinger bosons and SU(2) duality

The next step in the prepotential formulation is the introduction of Schwinger bosons.
We will reduce our discussion at this stage to
G=\mathrm{SU}(2)
, but the approach can be generalized to different gauge groups (see, e.g.,
[
95
,
54
,
72
]
for discussions of the prepotential formulation with
G=\mathrm{SU}(3)
).
The space
\mathcal{H}_{\text{SB}}
can be thought of as the Hilbert space of two bosonic species, the Schwinger bosons described by a doublet of operators
a^{A}
,
A=1,2
satisfying two copies of the canonical commutation relations (CCR) algebra

[a^{A},a^{\dagger}_{B}]=\delta^{A}_{B}\,.

(158)

At this point we will adopt the conventions that upper indices correspond to the fundamental
\mathrm{SU}(2)
representation, lower indices correspond to the antifundamental, and the Einstein summation convention contracting upper and lower indices.
Indeed, the space of Schwinger bosons carries a representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{su}(2)
given by

X\mapsto a^{\dagger}Xa(=a^{\dagger}_{A}X^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}a^{B})\,.

(159)

The isomorphism is then implemented through the identification

\displaystyle\ket{j,m}\equiv\ket{n_{1},n_{2}}\coloneq\frac{\quantity(a^\dagger_1)^{n_{1}}\quantity(a^\dagger_2)^{n_{2}}}{\sqrt{n_{1}!n_{2}!}}\ket{0},\hskip 11.74988ptn_{1}=j+m,\hskip 11.74988ptn_{2}=j-m\,,

(160)

with
\ket{0}
the bosonic vacuum of the theory.
On the left hand side of the
\equiv
sign, we are expressing states in a “spin” convention, where the first entry determines the total spin of the representation and the second its
z
-component.
On the right hand side, we instead represent the state in using an “occupation” number convention, where the first entry determines the total number of
a^{\dagger}_{1}
bosons and the second the total number of
a^{\dagger}_{2}
bosons.
Although ambiguous, we hope that the reader can identify which convention is being used through the context and the variable names inside of the kets.

One of the fundamental simplifying features of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
is that all representations are self-dual
V_{j}\cong V_{j}^{*}
.
This can be made manifest by noting that we can raise indices using the totally antisymmetric symbol
\epsilon^{AB}
.
We will define
\epsilon_{AB}=-\epsilon^{AB}
so that
\epsilon_{AB}\epsilon^{BC}=\delta^{C}_{A}
.
By convention we will take
\epsilon^{12}=1
and the contraction of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
doublets to be
12
12

12

The reader more familiar with index manipulation in general relativity might find this odd.
In our case, since we are raising and lowering indices with an antisymmetric symbol, we have
\psi_{A}\phi^{A}=-\psi^{A}\phi_{A}
.

\psi\phi\coloneq\psi_{A}\phi^{A}=\psi_{A}\epsilon^{AB}\phi_{B}\,.

(161)

We can then write the explicit formula of the isomorphism of every representation and its dual by

\displaystyle V_{j}^{*}

\displaystyle\rightarrow V_{j}

(162)

\displaystyle\bra{0}\frac{\quantity(a^1)^{j+m}\quantity(a^2)^{j-m}}{\sqrt{(j+m)!(j-m)!}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{\quantity(a^{\dagger 1})^{j+m}\quantity(a^{\dagger 2})^{j-m}}{\sqrt{(j+m)!(j-m)!}}\ket{0}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\bra{j,m}

\displaystyle\mapsto(-1)^{j-m}\ket{j,-m}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\bra{n_{1},n_{2}}

\displaystyle\mapsto(-1)^{n_{2}}\ket{n_{2},n_{1}}\,.

This will allow us to implement the prepotential formulation without the choice of an orientation.
Indeed, now we have an isomorphism

L^{2}(\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G}))\cong\bigoplus_{r\in\hat{G}}V_{r}\otimes V_{r}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\text{SB}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\text{SB}}\,,

(163)

where the first factor is supported in
h_{1}
and the second in
h_{2}
, given by

\displaystyle\bra{u}(\ket{j,m_{1}}\otimes\ket{j,m_{2}})=(-1)^{j+m_{1}}\matrixelement{j,-m_1}{r_j(u(h_1,h_2))}{j,m_2}\,.

(164)

Putting these ingredients together we obtain the prepotential formulation of the Hilbert space

\mathfrak{H}(\mathrm{G})=\mathcal{H}_{\text{SB}}^{\otimes H}\supseteq\mathcal{H}(\mathrm{G})\,,

(165)

obtained by tensoring the Schwinger boson Hilbert space on each half-link.
The action of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
on each half-link (
159
) extends the definition of the electric operators to the Hilbert space of this formulation

-iE_{X}(h)\coloneq a^{\dagger}(h)Xa(h)\,.

(166)

The index structure of the Schwinger bosons reflects the commutator with these

\displaystyle\quantity[-iE_X(h_1),a^A(h_2)]

\displaystyle=-X^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}a^{B}(h_{2})\delta_{h_{1}h_{2}}\mbox{\hskip 11.74988ptand\hskip 11.74988pt}

(167)

\displaystyle\quantity[-iE_X(h_1),a^\dagger_A(h_2)]

\displaystyle=a^{\dagger}_{B}(h_{2})X^{{{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}}\delta_{h_{1}h_{2}}\,.

The commutation relations with other operators can be read from the placement of its physical indices and their support.

While we have technically enlarged the Hilbert space, it is now very simple to characterize the gauge invariant states.
Indeed, states in this Hilbert space are spanned by monomials in the creation operators
13
13

13

We note that not all of the half-links in this notation have to be different.
The same can be said for the indices.

a^{\dagger}_{A_{1}}(h_{1})\cdots a^{\dagger}_{A_{n}}(h_{n})\ket{0}\,.

(168)

The transformation properties of such monomials are easily read from their index structure.
Furthermore, the gauge invariant linear combinations can be immediately constructed by demanding that the resulting state satisfies the property that, at each vertex, all of the half-links attached to it have indices that are fully contracted.

B.4.3
Loop-String-Hadron operators

A particularly convenient way of describing these gauge invariant states is obtained by collecting the creation and annihilation operators into a single operator

\displaystyle A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}(h)

\displaystyle\coloneq\begin{cases}a^{\dagger A}(h)&a=+\\
a^{A}(h)&a=-\par\end{cases}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{2}^{\dagger}(h)&a^{1}(h)\\
-a_{1}^{\dagger}(h)&a^{2}(h)\end{pmatrix}^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}\,.

(169)

In terms of these, the commutation relations become

[A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}(h_{1}),A^{{{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}(h_{2})]=\epsilon^{AB}g_{ab}\delta_{h_{1},h_{2}},\hskip 11.74988ptg=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 0 & -1\\ -1 & 0\endlx@physics@matrix)\,.

(170)

This
g
is the metric of
1+1
Minkowski space in lightcone coordinates, indicating that the
a
index is in the vector representation of this Poincaré group.
Since we are working with complex representations, this is equivalent to the vector representation of
\mathrm{SO}(2)=\mathrm{U}(1)
, which is the Abelian gauge symmetry associated with the Abelian Gauss law.
In this representation we can choose the convention
a=+
objects transform like
z=x+iy
while
a=-
objects transform like
z=x-iy
, so that it acts by

A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}(h)\mapsto e^{ia\phi}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}(h)\,.

(171)

Thus the associated charge counts the number of Schwinger bosons created by the operator.

With this we can form the loop operators

\mathcal{L}_{ab}(h_{1},h_{2})\coloneq A^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.4257pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.4257pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.64606pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.37007pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Aa}}}(h_{1})A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}(h_{2})\,,

(172)

obtained by contraction of the physical indices.
Therefore, as long as
\pi(h_{1})=\pi(h_{2})
, i.e. that
(h_{1},h_{2})
is a corner, this loop operator is gauge invariant.
On the other hand, the
\mathrm{U}(1)
charge of such an operator is
a
on
h_{1}
and
b
on
h_{2}
(and they add up if
h_{1}=h_{2}
).
These behave simply under change of order of the edges

\mathcal{L}_{ab}(h_{1},h_{2})=-\mathcal{L}_{ba}(h_{2},h_{1})+2g_{ab}\delta_{h_{1},h_{2}}\,,

(173)

and conjugation

\mathcal{L}_{ab}(h_{1},h_{2})^{\dagger}=-ab\mathcal{L}^{ba}(h_{2},h_{1})\,,

(174)

and form a closed Lie algebra
14
14

14

The table of commutation relations found in
[
45
]
can be summarized in a similar fashion if one creates a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded version of the field
A
that takes fermionic values on vertices and bosonic on half-links.

\displaystyle\quantity[\mathcal{L}_{ab}(h_1,h_2),\mathcal{L}_{cd}(h_3,h_4)]=

\displaystyle g_{ad}\delta_{h_{1},h_{4}}\mathcal{L}_{cb}(h_{3},h_{2})-g_{bc}\delta_{h_{2},h_{3}}\mathcal{L}_{ad}(h_{1},h_{4})

(175)

\displaystyle+g_{bd}\delta_{h_{2},h_{4}}\mathcal{L}_{ac}(h_{1},h_{3})-g_{ac}\delta_{h_{1},h_{3}}\mathcal{L}_{db}(h_{4},h_{2})\,.

Let us look at some particular examples of such operators that will be relevant for us.
For
h_{1}\neq h_{2}
, the operator

\mathcal{L}_{++}(h_{1},h_{2})=a^{\dagger}(h_{1})a^{\dagger}(h_{2})=-\mathcal{L}_{++}(h_{2},h_{1})\,,

(176)

precisely provides the gauge invariant linear combinations of creation operators.
It is thus useful to define a set of oriented corners
\mathcal{C}\subseteq C
which is a subset of the set of all corners such that for each corner
(h_{1},h_{2})
at a vertex
p\in\Gamma
either
(h_{1},h_{2})\in\mathcal{C}
,
(h_{2},h_{1})\in\mathcal{C}
but not both.
In other words, this is an orientation of the corners of the graph.
We then have

\displaystyle\Span\left.\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(c)}(c)\quantity{\left.\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(c)}(c)\ket{0} \right|n:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}}\right|n:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}=\bigcap_{p\in\Gamma,X\in\mathfrak{g}}\ker G_{X}(p)\,.

(177)

If
h_{1}=h_{2}=h
, the only non-trivial operator we obtain is the number operator

N(h)\coloneq\mathcal{L}_{+-}(h,h)=a^{\dagger}(h)a(h)\,.

(178)

In terms of the identification (
160
), this operator measures
2j=n_{1}+n_{2}
.
Accordingly, the Casimir of this representation is

\mathbf{E}^{2}(h)\coloneq\frac{N(h)}{2}\quantity(\frac{N(h)}{2}+1)\,.

(179)

In this equation we have chosen the vector of Pauli matrices
i{\bf\it\sigma}/2
as a basis of
\mathfrak{su}(2)
. This is orthonormal and yields the electric operators

\mathbf{E}(h)=E_{i{\bf\it\sigma}/2}(h)=-\frac{1}{2}a^{\dagger}(h){\bf\it\sigma}a(h)\,.

(180)

In light of the Abelian enlargement (
157
), the physical Hilbert space must be further constrained to satisfy
N(h_{1})=N(h_{2})
for every undirected link
\{h_{1},h_{2}\}\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.09999pt}{$\displaystyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.58333pt}{$\displaystyle{\sigma}$}}{\raisebox{4.09999pt}{$\textstyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.58333pt}{$\textstyle{\sigma}$}}{\raisebox{2.87pt}{$\scriptstyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.80832pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sigma}$}}{\raisebox{2.05pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sigma}$}}}
.
In other words, we must introduce the Abelian constraints

G(\{h_{1},h_{2}\})\coloneq|N(h_{1})-N(h_{2})|\,,

(181)

in terms of which the physical Hilbert space is now given by the set of all quantum numbers
N(h)
subject to the constraint
N(h_{1})-N(h_{2})=0

\mathfrak{h}(\Gamma)=\quantity(\bigcap_{p\in\Gamma}\ker G(p))\cap\quantity(\bigcap_{\{h_1,h_2\}\in\faktor{H}{\sigma}}\ker G(\{h_1,h_2\}))\,.

(182)

The first intersection, corresponding to the invariant states under the non-Abelian Gauss’ law, is resolved by creating states through the loop operators.
The second, corresponding to the invariant states under the Abelian one, by cycling through every undirected link
\{h_{1},h_{2}\}\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.09999pt}{$\displaystyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.58333pt}{$\displaystyle{\sigma}$}}{\raisebox{4.09999pt}{$\textstyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.58333pt}{$\textstyle{\sigma}$}}{\raisebox{2.87pt}{$\scriptstyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.80832pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sigma}$}}{\raisebox{2.05pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{H}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.29166pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sigma}$}}}
and ensuring the numbers of loop creation operators with support on
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
coincides.

With these operators in mind, it is instructive to look at particular examples of the commutation relations.
Let us start looking at the relations on a single corner
(h_{1},h_{2})
with
h_{1}\neq h_{2}
.
First, we note that

[N(h_{1}),\mathcal{L}_{ab}(h_{1},h_{2})]=\delta_{a+}\mathcal{L}_{+b}(h_{1},h_{2})-\delta_{a-}\mathcal{L}_{-b}(h_{1},h_{2})\,,

(183)

so that the number operator as expected measures the number of creations or annihilations at the half-link.
Furthermore, unlike a harmonic oscillator, the commutator of creation and annihilation operators is not proportional to the identity

[\mathcal{L}_{--}(h_{1},h_{2}),\mathcal{L}_{++}(h_{1},h_{2})]=N(h_{1})+N(h_{2})+2\,.

(184)

This is of course a consequence of the fact that our loop operators are bilinears in the Schwinger boson operators.
Finally, let us remark that the mixed operators commute with the creation and annihilation operators

\displaystyle{[}\mathcal{L}_{+-}(h_{1},h_{2}),\mathcal{L}_{++}(h_{1},h_{2}){]}

\displaystyle=0

(185)

\displaystyle{[}\mathcal{L}_{+-}(h_{1},h_{2}),\mathcal{L}_{--}(h_{1},h_{2}){]}

\displaystyle=0

It is also interesting to study the commutation relations obtained when one introduces another corner
(h_{3},h_{1})
with
h_{3}\neq h_{1}
and
h_{3}\neq h_{2}
.
In that case we have

[\mathcal{L}_{ab}(h_{1},h_{2}),\mathcal{L}_{cd}(h_{3},h_{1})]=-g_{ad}\mathcal{L}_{bc}(h_{2},h_{3})\,.

(186)

In particular, if the indices at the overlapping half-link match, the operators commute.
Otherwise, we obtain commutators like

[\mathcal{L}_{--}(h_{1},h_{2}),\mathcal{L}_{++}(h_{3},h_{1})]=\mathcal{L}_{-+}(h_{2},h_{3})\,.

(187)

This show that switching flux from
h_{3}
to
h_{2}
can be achieved by anlihilation of flux on
(h_{3},h_{1})
and creation of flux on
(h_{1},h_{2})
.

B.4.4
Wilson line operators

Let us finish by discussing the construction of the Wilson line operators
U^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}(h_{1},h_{2})
in terms of prepotentials.
For this, let us take advantage of the fact that the
\mathrm{U}(1)
and the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
representations appearing in
A
have the same dimension to define

A^{{{\dagger a}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.69067pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.69067pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.47368pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.08675pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}}(h)\coloneq(A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}(h))^{\dagger}=\begin{pmatrix}a^{2}(h)&-a^{1}(h)\\
a_{1}^{\dagger}(h)&a_{2}^{\dagger}(h)\end{pmatrix}^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}}\,.

(188)

with the index placement reflecting the transformation behavior of this operator.
Explicitly, one can check that

A^{{{\dagger a}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.69067pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.69067pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.47368pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.08675pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}}(h)=aA^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}(h)\,,

(189)

raising and lowering indices using
g_{ab}
.
With this one can then prove that

(A(h)A^{\dagger}(h))^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\coloneq A(h)^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}A^{\dagger}(h)^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}=\delta^{A}_{B}(N(h)+1)\,.

(190)

This suggests the introduction of a new Wilson line operator on each half-link given by

U(h)^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}\coloneq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N(h)+1}}A(h)^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}\,,

(191)

which then satisfies

U(h)^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}U^{\dagger}(h)^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}=\delta^{A}_{B}\,.

(192)

In fact, this operator is “unitary,” for

U^{\dagger}(h)^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}}U(h)^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}=\delta^{a}_{b}\,.

(193)

This can be checked by noticing that

A^{\dagger}(h)^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}}A(h)^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}=(N(h)+1+a)\delta^{a}_{b}\,,

(194)

and that

\mathcal{O}f(N(h))=f(N(h)-o)\mathcal{O}\,

(195)

with
o
the
\mathrm{U}(1)
charge of the operator
\mathcal{O}
at
h
.

Now consider an edge
(h_{1},h_{2})
. We wish to obtain a unitary operator
U^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}(h_{1},h_{2})
out of the operators
U^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}(h_{1})
and
U^{{{\dagger b}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.76804pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.76804pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64406pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.66916pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}}(h_{2})
.
This can then be done by multiplying the two operators together and contracting the
a
and
b
indices (note that these operators commute since they are localized in different half-links).
This naive approach would however be invariant under the
U(1)
action acting simultaneously on
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
because there are no more
U(1)
indices.
In other words, the operator would commute with
N(h_{1})+N(h_{2})
.
This is because of the placement of the indices - whenever one of the operators creates a boson on a half-link, the other destroys one on the other half-link.
Instead, the Abelian Gauss law demands that physical operators commute with
N(h_{1})-N(h_{2})
, so that whenever a boson is created in a half-link, another boson should be created on the other.
Thus, instead of contracting the
a
and
b
indices with one on top and one below, we should contract them with the same placement.
In order to keep consistency with the Einstein summation convention, we can then introduce a matrix
t
that does that

U^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}(h_{1},h_{2})=U^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}(h_{1})t^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}U^{{{\dagger b}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.76804pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.76804pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[7.64406pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.66916pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}(h_{2})\,.

(196)

Any off-diagonal matrix would do to obtain something that satisfies the Abelian Gauss law.
If we further want a unitary result, we should choose a unitary
t
, which would then guarantee

\displaystyle U^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}(h_{1},h_{2})U^{{{\dagger B}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.09596pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.09596pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.29216pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.26312pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{C}}}(h_{1},h_{2})=U^{{{\dagger A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[12.63515pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[12.63515pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.01247pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.01666pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}(h_{1},h_{2})U^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.73022pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.59392pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{D}}}(h_{1},h_{2})=\delta^{A}_{C}\,.

(197)

A final restriction, which fixes the form of this matrix is that the result should have unit determinant.
At this stage, the meaning of this statement is not clear, for the elements of
U^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}(h_{1},h_{2})
are themselves operators.
The meaning is acquired by the fact that the components commute, so that the requirement that

1=U^{{{1}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00172pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00172pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{1}}}(h_{1},h_{2})U^{{{2}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00172pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00172pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{2}}}(h_{1},h_{2})-U^{{{1}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00172pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00172pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{2}}}(h_{1},h_{2})U^{{{2}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00172pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00172pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{1}}}(h_{1},h_{2})\,,

(198)

becomes independent of the operator ordering.
This is proven in
Sec.

B.4.5
.
Using the techniques developed there, one can show that the determinant condition is equivalent to taking
t
to have unit determinant.
This therefore reduces the possible choice of
t
down to

t^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 0 & e^{i\theta}\\
-e^{-i\theta} & 0\endlx@physics@matrix)^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}.

(199)

In general, one can choose a different
\theta
for every link, so that
t^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}=t^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}(h_{1},h_{2})
.
One however would like to impose the condition that
U(h_{1},h_{2})^{-1}(=U(h_{1},h_{2})^{\dagger})=U(h_{2},h_{1})
.
We must then have

t(h_{1},h_{2})^{\dagger}=t(h_{2},h_{1}).

(200)

Given our specific form for
t
, we conclude that
t(h_{2},h_{1})=-t(h_{1},h_{2})
.

In practice, for definiteness one can orient the graph.
Along the positive oriented links one can absorb the
\theta
dependence in the
\mathrm{U}(1)
gauge invariance.
This amounts to chosing

t^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 0 & 1\\
-1 & 0\endlx@physics@matrix)^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}.

(201)

for those edges and its conjugate for the negatively oriented ones.
For this choice we have
T^{ab}=\delta^{ab}
.
Thus, on the positively oriented links we can take

\displaystyle U^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}(h_{1},h_{2})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N(h_{1})+1}}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}(h_{1})A^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.88652pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.88652pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.92575pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.61653pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Ba}}}(h_{2})\frac{1}{\sqrt{N(h_{2})+1}}\,,

(202)

and its negative for the negatively oriented links.
Even though the
a
’s are not one on top and one below, a summation is implied in this formula.
This is done to minimize the factors of
\delta^{ab}
appearing in our formulae.

Not being gauge invariants, it is not surprising that generic Wilson line operators themselves are not immediately expressible in terms of LSH operators.
Nevertheless, gauge invariant combinations built out of them can.
In order to do this, it is useful to define LSH Wilson lines.
Unlike, the usual Wilson lines, these are supported on corners instead of links.
Accordingly, instead of transforming under the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
copies at the source and target of a link, the transform under the
\mathrm{U}(1)
copies at the source and target of the corner.
They are obtained by contracting in a
\mathrm{SU}(2)
invariant way the indices of the Wilson line operators on the half-links of the corner.
Thus, given a corner
(h_{1},h_{2})
we define

\displaystyle U^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}(h_{1},h_{2})=U^{{{\dagger a}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.69067pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.69067pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.47368pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.08675pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}}(h_{1})U^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}(h_{2})=\begin{cases}a\frac{1}{\sqrt{N(h_{2})+1}}\mathcal{L}^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}(h_{1},h_{2})\frac{1}{\sqrt{N(h_{1})+1}}&h_{1}\neq h_{2}\\
\delta^{a}_{b}&h_{1}=h_{2}\,.\end{cases}

(203)

These are known as vertex factors in
[

]
.

As an example of their use, consider a pair of corners
(h_{1},h_{2})
and
(h_{3},h_{4})
with
\pi(h_{3})=\pi(h_{2})
.
Then we have

\displaystyle d}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.54239pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.54239pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.2695pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{C}}}(h_{4})\,.

More generally, let
\gamma
be a trajectory.
Let us define the trajectory

as follows.

\displaystyle . This amounts to setting

once, we can push the first factor all the way to the end and recover yet another corner Wilson line operator. We then have

\displaystyle\tr U(\gamma)=

\displaystyle t^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}(\gamma(n))U^{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}(\gamma_{c})t^{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.59233pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.59233pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34946pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.18604pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}(\gamma(1))U^{{{d}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.59233pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.59233pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34946pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.18604pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}(b(1),a(n))

(208)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}(\gamma(n))U^{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}(\tilde{\gamma})\,,

where
\tilde{\gamma}
is the corner trajectory that first goes through
(b(1),a(n))
and then through
\gamma_{c}
.
This is now fully expressed in the context of LSH operators.

Let us finish this section with a few comments on the application of this formulation.
First of all, the condition that
\gamma
only goes through
a(n)
once might seem rather restrictive at first.
In reality, since
\tr U(\gamma)
is invariant under shifts of the starting (which is also the finishing) point of
\gamma
, this is not the case.
Thus, in order to use this formula, we just need there to exist at least a single half-link that
\gamma
goes through once.
Second, let us remark that

\displaystyle t^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}(h_{1},h_{2})U^{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}(h_{2},h_{3})=\pm\delta^{ab}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N(h_{3})+1}}\mathcal{L}_{bc}(h_{2},h_{3})\frac{1}{\sqrt{N(h_{2})+1}}\,,

(209)

where the sign is determined by whether
(h_{1},h_{2})
is positively oriented or not.
Therefore, in practical applications the
t
matrices are there only to keep track of the sign, and one can immediately write everything in terms of loop operators with lower indices.

B.4.5
Proof of commutative property of Wilson line operator components

In this section we show that the components of the Wilson line operator commute.
To streamline this computation, let us introduce some notation that we will use taking advantage of the fact that for this the half-links
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
will be fixed.
First, we will suppress the labels associated to these.
In order to avoid confusion due to this, we will set

A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}(h_{1})=A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}\mbox{\hskip 11.74988ptand\hskip 11.74988pt}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}(h_{2})=B^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}\,.

(210)

We will also restrict to the subspace satisfying the Abelian Gauss law, so that

\mathcal{L}_{+-}(h_{1},h_{1})=\mathcal{L}_{+-}(h_{2},h_{2})=\mathcal{L}_{+-}=-\mathcal{L}_{+-}-2=N\,.

(211)

Finally, we will introduce the notation

\Phi(a)\coloneq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N+a+1}}\,.

(212)

With this notation in place, let us define a rescaled commutator that will be more comfortable to work with

C^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.73022pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.59392pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{D}}}\coloneq\Phi(0)^{-1}[U^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}},U^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.73022pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.59392pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{D}}}]\Phi(0)^{-1}\,.

(213)

Our main goal will be to show that this vanishes on the subspace satisfying the Abelian Gauss law.
Let us write

U^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}=T^{ae}\Phi(0)A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}B^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.28616pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.28616pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[10.35297pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.35208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Be}}}\Phi(0),\hskip 11.74988ptT^{ae}\coloneq\sum_{b=\pm}bt^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}g^{be}\,,

(214)

using (
191
) and (
189
).
The only property we will need of the matrix
T^{ab}
is that it is diagonal since both
t^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.14026pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.14026pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.98085pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.95563pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{e}}}
and
g^{be}
are off-diagonal.
With this we have that

\displaystyle\Phi(0)^{-1}U^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}U^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.73022pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.59392pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{D}}}\Phi(0)^{-1}

\displaystyle=T^{ae}T^{cf}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}B_{Be}\Phi(0)^{2}A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}B_{Df}

(215)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a}\Phi(-a)^{2}T^{ae}T^{cf}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}B_{Be}B_{Df}\,.

In the last line we used the fact that the
A
’s and
B
’s commute since their support does not overlap, (
195
), and made the sum over
a
explicit since we broke the summation convention. Then the commutator is equal to

\displaystyle C^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.73022pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.59392pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{D}}}=\sum_{a}\Phi(-a)^{2}T^{ae}T^{cf}\quantity(A\indices{^A_a}A\indices{^C_c}B_{Be}B_{Df}-A\indices{^C_a}A\indices{^A_c}B_{De}B_{Bf})\,.

(216)

Next, we note that the term in parenthesis, which is quartic in Schwinger bosons, can be reduced to a bilinear with functions of
N
as coefficients.
For this one adds and subtracts
A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}B_{Be}B_{Df}
, so that the term in parenthesis reduces to the sum
