\displaystyle(A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}-A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}})B_{Be}B_{Df}+A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}(B_{Be}B_{Df}-B_{De}B_{Bf})\,.

(217)

We claim that the terms in parenthesis are functions of
N
.
Focusing on the one in the first row, the
A\leftrightarrow C
antisymmetry guarantees that

A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}-A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}=\epsilon^{AC}(A^{{{1}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00172pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}A^{{{2}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00172pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}-A^{{{2}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00172pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}A^{{{1}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00172pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}})=\epsilon^{AC}(A^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.20317pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.20317pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.55536pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.32008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{2a}}}A^{{{2}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00172pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}+A^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.20317pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.20317pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.55536pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.32008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{1a}}}A^{{{1}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.46255pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.00172pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}})=\epsilon^{AC}\mathcal{L}_{ac}\,.

(218)

Including the analogous result for the second row and using the diagonality of the
T^{ae}
matrices, the commutator takes the form

C^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.73022pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.59392pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{D}}}=\epsilon^{AC}M_{BD}(B)+\epsilon_{DB}M^{CA}(A)\,,

(219)

where

M^{CA}(A)=\sum_{a}\Phi(-a)^{2}T^{ae}T^{cf}\mathcal{L}_{ef}A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}\,.

(220)

We further claim that
M^{CA}
is itself a function of the number operator.
For this one can start by noting that the
a=c
terms vanish identically because
\mathcal{L}_{ef}
would vanish.
Therefore, there are only two terms in the contractions of
M^{CA}
.
Furthermore, both are proportional to
T^{++}T^{--}
.
Dividing this common factor out, the
a=+
term is

\Phi(-1)^{2}\mathcal{L}_{+-}A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.94176pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.94176pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.04343pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26668pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{+}}}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}=A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.94176pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.94176pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.04343pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26668pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{+}}}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}\,.

(221)

In the last equal sign we used (
211
).
Similarly, the
a=-
term is

\Phi(+1)^{2}\mathcal{L_{-+}}A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.94176pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.94176pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.04343pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26668pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{+}}}=-A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.94176pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.94176pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.04343pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26668pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{+}}}\,.

(222)

Therefore

\displaystyle\frac{M^{CA}(A)}{T^{++}T^{--}}

\displaystyle=A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.94176pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.94176pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.04343pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26668pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{+}}}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}-A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.94176pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.94176pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.04343pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26668pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{+}}}

(223)

\displaystyle=A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.94176pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.94176pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.04343pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26668pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{+}}}A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}-A^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.94176pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.94176pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.04343pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.26668pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{+}}}A^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}+\epsilon^{CA}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{CA}(N+1)\,.

Inserting these relations we conclude that

C^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.73022pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.59392pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{D}}}=\epsilon^{AC}\epsilon_{DB}(N+1)+\epsilon_{DB}\epsilon^{CA}(N+1)=0\,,

(224)

concluding our proof.

The same technique employed in this proof can be used to show that

\Phi(0)^{-1}\det U\Phi(0)^{-1}=T^{++}T^{--}(N+1)\,.

(225)

Thus, the unit determinant condition demands that

T^{++}T^{--}=1.

(226)

This is equivalent to demanding that
t
has unit determinant.

Appendix C
Relating Theories on Different Graphs

In this section we will attempt to formalize the graph manipulations required in the main body of the paper. In
Sec.

C.1
and
Sec.

C.2
, we give a general description of graph manipulations and whether they yield kinematically equivalent physical representations.
The equivalences are made explicit in these sections as well. The former deals with the classical setting.
The latter with the quantum mechanical one. In
Sec.

C.3
these general theorems are put in practice for the two manipulations required in the main body of the paper.
In
Sec.

C.3.1
, virtual point splitting is discussed. This is fundamental in transforming petals into leaves and flowers into branches.
We show that graphs can always be manipulated to become fully trivalent.
We then give proofs for the normalization of the states and LSH operators given in
[
45
]
.
In
Sec.

C.3.2
, we discuss the flower construction in the context of maximal tree gauge fixing.
Finally,
Sec.

C.4
lifts the kinematical equivalences to dynamical ones by showing how the ingredients for the engineering of Hamiltonians are translated through these manipulations. This is first done in the context of the original Kogut-Susskind formulation
Sec.

C.4.1
.
In the context of maximal tree gauge fixing, we simply restate and give proofs for the results found in
[
52
]
in our context.
We also provide analogous results for the case of point splitting.
We then show how to rewrite the ingredients in the language of LSH in
Sec.

C.4.2
.

C.1
Classical aspects

In this paper we will find ourselves multiple times with the following situation.
Consider a set of trajectories
R\subseteq T
on
\mathrm{G}
.
Then one can define a new graph
\mathrm{G}_{R}
as follows.
The vertices of
\mathrm{G}_{R}
will be given by the start and endpoints of the trajectories in
R

\displaystyle\Gamma_{R}\coloneq

\displaystyle\Set{p\in\Gamma}{\exists\gamma\in R:s(\gamma)=p\hskip 11.74988pt\mbox{or}\hskip 11.74988ptt(\gamma)=p}

(227)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Set{s(\gamma)}{\gamma\in R}\cup\Set{t(\gamma)}{\gamma\in R}\,.

The half-links of
\mathrm{G}_{R}
will be given by two copies of
R
.
This means that we will define two half-links
h_{\gamma}^{(s)}
and
h_{\gamma}^{(t)}\in H_{R}
to each trajectory
\gamma\in R
.
If one wants to, it is useful to have the picture in mind that
h_{\gamma}^{(s)}
corresponds to the first half of
\gamma
while
h_{\gamma}^{(t)}
to the second half, so that the link
(h_{\gamma}^{(t)},h_{\gamma}^{(s)})
in
\mathrm{G}_{R}
is simply the trajectory
\gamma
.
The attachment map is defined by the declaring that each of these pairs is attached to one another, i.e.

\sigma_{R}(h_{\gamma}^{(s)})=h_{\gamma}^{(t)}\mbox{\hskip 11.74988ptand\hskip 11.74988pt}\sigma_{R}(h_{\gamma}^{(t)})=h_{\gamma}^{(s)}\,.

(228)

Finally, the anchoring map
\pi_{R}
is given by

\pi_{R}(h_{\gamma}^{(s)})=s(\gamma)\mbox{\hskip 11.74988ptand\hskip 11.74988pt}\pi_{R}(h_{\gamma}^{(t)})=t(\gamma)\,.

(229)

It is useful to note that
\mathrm{G}_{R}
is actually independent of the orientation of the trajectories in
R
.
However, in order to make the notation as simple as possible, we have decided to not make this independence manifest.

Whenever we find ourselves in this situation, every field configuration on
\mathrm{G}
naturally induces a field configuration on
\mathrm{G}_{R}
, according to the intution presented above when defining the half-links.
This defines a map

S:\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G}_{R})\,,

(230)

given by identifying the Wilson line on the link
(h_{\gamma}^{(t)},h_{\gamma}^{(s)})
with that on
\gamma

S(u)(h_{\gamma}^{(t)},h_{\gamma}^{(s)})\coloneq u(\gamma)\,,

(231)

for all
\gamma\in R
.
Let us spell out some of the properties of this map.

First of all, we may wonder whether
S
is surjective.
This need not be the case if the trajectories in
R
are not different enough.
For example, if there is a
\gamma\in R
such that
\gamma^{-1}\in R
as well, then the field configurations in the image of
S
have Wilson lines on
(h_{\gamma}^{(t)},h_{\gamma}^{(s)})
and
(h_{\gamma^{-1}}^{(t)},h_{\gamma^{-1}}^{(s)})
that are inversely related to one another.
However, in all of the examples that we will be interested in, the set of trajectories
R
satisfies a property that will guarantee the surjectivity of
S
:
for each trajectory
\gamma\in R
, there is a link
\gamma(i)
that is not traversed, in any direction, by any other trajectory in
R
15
15

15

This assumption is not true in the
R
one would choose to recover the results of
[
42
]
.
Still, one can show that in that case
S
is surjective and the rest of our proofs carry through.
.
Of course, there may be several links that satisfy this property for each trajectory in
R
.
Let us however choose one of them, defining a map

f:R\rightarrow E\,.

(232)

Explicitly, this map has the properties:

1.

for each
\gamma\in R
of length
n
, there is an
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, such that
\gamma(i)=f(\gamma)
,

2.

for each
\gamma\in R
, there is no
\gamma^{\prime}\in R
for which there is an
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, with
n
the length of
\gamma^{\prime}
, for which
f(\gamma)=\gamma^{\prime}(i)
or
f(\gamma)=\gamma^{\prime}(i)^{-1}
.

We will show that a choice of such an
f
guarantees the surjectivity of
S
by constructing from it a right inverse

\iota:\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G}_{R})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G})\,,

(233)

to
S
.
The choice of notation chosen for it is meant to remind the reader that, given that
\iota
has a left inverse, namely
S
, it must be injective.
Physically, one should thing of
\iota
as a
choice
of interpretation of each field configuration on
\mathrm{G}_{R}
as a field configuration on
G
.
Note that, while
S
is canonically defined from the choice of trajectories
R
, the embedding
\iota
is not.

The definition of
\iota
relies on utilizing each
f(\gamma)
to store the value of the Wilson line on
\gamma
.
Given that
f(\gamma)
is not traversed by any other trajectories, there are no conflicts in this procedure.
The rest of the Wilson lines can then be set to the identity.
Explicitly, consider a
u\in\mathrm{G}_{R}
.
Define

\iota(u)(e)\coloneq\begin{cases}u(h_{\gamma}^{(t)},h_{\gamma}^{(s)})&e=f(\gamma),\hskip 11.74988pt\gamma\in R,\\
1&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

(234)

The conditions that
f
satisfy guarantee that
\iota(u)
is well-defined and that
S(\iota(u))=u
.

Both
\mathrm{G}
and
\mathrm{G}_{R}
are acted on by their own gauge groups
G^{\Gamma}
and
G^{\Gamma_{R}}
, respectively.
Given that
\Gamma_{R}\subseteq\Gamma
, there is a surjective map
S:G^{\Gamma}\rightarrow G^{\Gamma_{R}}
between these gauge groups simply given by restricting the gauge transformations on
\mathrm{G}
, so that

S(g)(p)\coloneq g(p)\,,

(235)

for all
p\in\Gamma_{R}
.
We have used the same name for it as for the map
S:\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G}_{R})
, given that they play similar roles.
Any confusion should be dispelled by looking at the argument of the function.

The map
S
is equivariant with respect to these group actions

S(g\triangleright u)=S(g)\triangleright S(u)\,.

(236)

In order to see this, one simply notes that

\displaystyle S(g\triangleright u)(h_{\gamma}^{(t)},h_{\gamma}^{(s)})=

\displaystyle(g\triangleright u)(\gamma)=g(t(\gamma))u(\gamma)g(s(\gamma))^{-1}

(237)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(\pi_{R}(h_{\gamma}^{(t)}))S(u)(h_{\gamma}^{(t)},h_{\gamma}^{(s)})g(\pi_{R}(h_{\gamma}^{(s)}))^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(S(g)\triangleright S(u))(h_{\gamma}^{(t)},h_{\gamma}^{(s)})\,,

for all
\gamma\in R
.
This property is a reflection of the naturalness of
S
.
On the other hand,
\iota
, being non-canonical, does not satisfy an analogous property in general.
It is curious to note that in both of the cases that we will consider in this paper, it turns out that
\iota
is indeed equivariant.
We will however not use this fact and therefore will not dwell on this.

It will be useful from now on to go to a slightly more abstract scenario and consider the generic case where we have two classical systems with configuration spaces given by
X
and
Y
, on which the groups
G
and
H
act respectively.
Suppose we have a surjective map
S:X\rightarrow Y
, which is equivariant with respect to a surjective map
S:G\rightarrow H
.
Let
\iota:Y\rightarrow X
be a right inverse for
S:X\rightarrow Y
.
Let us show that
[S]:{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.09999pt}{$\displaystyle{X}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.09999pt}{$\displaystyle{G}$}}{\raisebox{4.09999pt}{$\textstyle{X}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.09999pt}{$\textstyle{G}$}}{\raisebox{2.87pt}{$\scriptstyle{X}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.87pt}{$\scriptstyle{G}$}}{\raisebox{2.05pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{X}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.05pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{G}$}}}\rightarrow{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.09999pt}{$\displaystyle{Y}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.09999pt}{$\displaystyle{H}$}}{\raisebox{4.09999pt}{$\textstyle{Y}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.09999pt}{$\textstyle{H}$}}{\raisebox{2.87pt}{$\scriptstyle{Y}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.87pt}{$\scriptstyle{H}$}}{\raisebox{2.05pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{Y}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.05pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{H}$}}}
is an isomorphism if and only if every
x\in X
is
G
-equivalent to
\iota(S(x))
.
Note that the surjectivity of
S
already guarantees that
[S]
is surjective.
Therefore
[S]
being an isomorphism is equivalent to
[S]
being injective.

On the one hand, assume that
[S]
is injective.
Take
x\in X
.
Then we have that
S(x)=S(\iota(S(x)))
.
Thus, given that
[S]
is injective, we must have that
x\sim\iota(S(x))
.
On the other hand, now assume that every
x\in X
is
G
-equivalent to
\iota(S(x))
.
Further, assume that we have
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
such that
S(x_{1})
and
S(x_{2})
are
H
-equivalent.
To show injectivity of
[S]
, we need to show that
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are
G
-equivalent.
The key to see this is to note that, the equivalence of
S(x_{1})
and
S(x_{2})
, as well as the surjectiveness of
S:G\rightarrow H
, guarantees there is a
g\in G
such that

S(x_{2})=S(g)\triangleright S(x_{1})=S(g\triangleright x_{1})\,,

(238)

so that, without loss of generality, we can assume that
S(x_{2})=S(x_{1})
.
In that case, the equivalence of
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
is clear since

x_{1}\sim\iota(S(x_{1}))=\iota(S(x_{2}))\sim x_{2}\,.

(239)

Going back to our specific case of interest, this means that the theories on
\mathrm{G}
and
\mathrm{G}_{R}
are kinematically equivalent as long as every field
u\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G})
is gauge equivalent to
\iota(S(u))
.

C.2
Quantum aspects

Thinking back to our general scenario, the map
S:X\rightarrow Y
induces a linear map
S^{*}:L^{2}(Y)\rightarrow L^{2}(X)
which assigns to every wave function
\ket{\psi}\in L^{2}(Y)
a wave function
S^{*}\ket{\psi}\in L^{2}(X)
.
As mentioned above, in the cases that we will be interested in,
S
will always be surjective.
This guarantees that
S^{*}
is injective.
Indeed, the only
\ket{\psi}\in L^{2}(Y)
such that
S^{*}\ket{\psi}=0
, is
\ket{\psi}=0
.
To see this, consider
y\in Y
.
Then, since
S
is surjective, there is an
x\in X
such that
S(x)=y
.
We conclude that

\innerproduct{y}{\psi}=\innerproduct{S(x)}{\psi}=\matrixelement{x}{S^*}{\psi}=0\,.

(240)

Another face of the above is to note that the map
\iota^{*}:L^{2}(X)\rightarrow L^{2}(Y)
is now a left inverse to
S^{*}
, for

\matrixelement{y}{\iota^*S^*}{\psi}=\innerproduct{S(\iota(y))}{\psi}=\innerproduct{y}{\psi}\,.

(241)

The equivariance of
S
further guarantees that
S^{*}
restricts to a map between gauge invariant wave functions
S^{*}:L^{2}(Y)^{H}\rightarrow L^{2}(X)^{G}
, where

\displaystyle L^{2}(X)^{G}\coloneq\{

\displaystyle\ket{\psi}\in L^{2}(X)|\innerproduct{g\triangleright x}{\psi}=\innerproduct{x}{\psi}\hskip 11.74988pt\mbox{for all}\hskip 11.74988ptx\in X\mbox{\hskip 11.74988ptand\hskip 11.74988pt}g\in G\}\,.

(242)

This is because if
\ket{\psi}\in L^{2}(Y)^{H}
, then for all
x\in X
and
g\in G
, we have

\displaystyle\matrixelement{g\triangleright x}{S^*}{\psi}

\displaystyle=\innerproduct{S(g\triangleright x)}{\psi}=\innerproduct{S(g)\triangleright S( x)}{\psi}

(243)

\displaystyle=\innerproduct{S(x)}{\psi}=\matrixelement{x}{S^*}{\psi}\,,

so that indeed
S^{*}\ket{\psi}\in L^{2}(X)^{G}
.

In fact, whenever the induced map

[S]:{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.09999pt}{$\displaystyle{X}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.09999pt}{$\displaystyle{G}$}}{\raisebox{4.09999pt}{$\textstyle{X}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.09999pt}{$\textstyle{G}$}}{\raisebox{2.87pt}{$\scriptstyle{X}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.87pt}{$\scriptstyle{G}$}}{\raisebox{2.05pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{X}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.05pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{G}$}}}\rightarrow{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.09999pt}{$\displaystyle{Y}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.09999pt}{$\displaystyle{H}$}}{\raisebox{4.09999pt}{$\textstyle{Y}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.09999pt}{$\textstyle{H}$}}{\raisebox{2.87pt}{$\scriptstyle{Y}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.87pt}{$\scriptstyle{H}$}}{\raisebox{2.05pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{Y}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.05pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{H}$}}}

(244)

is an isomorphism, we will have that
S^{*}:L^{2}(Y)^{H}\rightarrow L^{2}(X)^{G}
is an isomorphism as well.
Of course, the restriction is injective because the full
S^{*}
was.
We thus only need to show that it is surjective.
For this, let us assume we have a gauge invariant
\ket{\psi}\in L^{2}(X)^{G}
.
Then we will argue that
\iota^{*}\ket{\psi}
is gauge invariant and satisfies
S^{*}\iota^{*}\ket{\psi}=\ket{\psi}
.

Tackling gauge invariance first, let
y\in Y
and
h\in H
.
Consider

\matrixelement{h\triangleright y}{\iota^*}{\psi}=\innerproduct{\iota(h\triangleright y)}{\psi}\,.

(245)

By surjectivity of
S
, we have
x\in X
and
g\in G
such that
S(x)=y
and
S(g)=h
.
Then, equivariance of
S
guarantees that

\iota(h\triangleright y)=\iota(S(g)\triangleright S(x))=\iota(S(g\triangleright x))\,.

(246)

Furthermore, since
[S]
is an isomorphism, we have

\iota(h\triangleright y)=\iota(S(g\triangleright x))\sim g\triangleright x\sim\iota(S(x))=\iota(y)\,.

(247)

Therefore, by gauge invariance of
\ket{\psi}
, we have

\matrixelement{h\triangleright y}{\iota^*}{\psi}=\innerproduct{\iota(y)}{\psi}=\matrixelement{y}{\iota^*}{\psi}\,,

(248)

proving that
\iota^{*}\ket{\psi}
is gauge invariant.

Now, we need to show that we indeed have
S^{*}\iota^{*}\ket{\psi}=\ket{\psi}
.
For this, let
x\in X
.
Then

\matrixelement{x}{S^*\iota^*}{\psi}=\innerproduct{\iota(S(x))}{\psi}\,.

(249)

Then, the bijectivity of
[S]
guarantees that
\iota(S(x))\sim x
, while the gauge invariance of
\ket{\psi}
then gives

\matrixelement{x}{S^*\iota^*}{\psi}=\innerproduct{x}{\psi}\,.

(250)

In other words, the classical equivalence described in the previous section lifts to a quantum mechanical one.

C.3
Examples

C.3.1
Virtual point splitting

A general difficulty associated to applying the LSH formulation in more than one dimension is that the gauge invariant states constructed with the loop operators (
176
) are not orthogonal to one another, even after choosing a preferred orientation for each corner.
So, while they span the whole gauge invariant Hilbert space, they satisfy linear relations known as Mandelstam constraints.
Indeed, consider a graph
\mathrm{G}
with a vertex
p\in\Gamma
of valency
4
, such as the one shown in
Fig.

24
.
Due to the identity
[
91
]

\displaystyle\epsilon^{AB}\epsilon^{CD}

\displaystyle(=\delta^{AC}\delta^{BD}-\delta^{AD}\delta^{BC}

(251)

\displaystyle=\delta^{AC}\delta^{BD}-\delta^{AB}\delta^{CD}+\delta^{AB}\delta^{CD}-\delta^{AD}\delta^{BC})

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{AD}\epsilon^{CB}+\epsilon^{AC}\epsilon^{BD}\,,

we have the linear relation of
Fig.

24
.
It is clear what the issue is: the configuration with a unit of gauge flux on every half-link of this vertex has three distinct interpretations in terms of the 6 non-trivial corners in a four-valent vertex.

Figure 24:
Mandelstam constraint between gauge invariant states obtained from the loop operators (
176
).

This problem of course extends to higher valency vertices.
If, on the other hand, we had a graph whose vertices had valency less than or equal to three, Mandelstam constraints would not be present, since no analog of
Eq.

251
can be written.
In this case, the loop operators would truly furnish a basis of the gauge invariant Hilbert space.
Fortunately, there is a generic method that one can employ to lower the valency of a vertex
p\in\mathrm{G}
while not changing the theory
[
96
]
.
The idea is to take two half-links
a
and
b
emerging from
p
and collapse their bases, leaving us with a new virtual unoriented link
\{e,f\}
and vertex
s
.
From this new vertex stems two further half-links
\tilde{a}
and
\tilde{b}
, which are the remainders of the original half-links
a
and
b
.
Let us denote the resulting graph by
\tilde{\mathrm{G}}
.
This construction is shown in
Fig.

25
.
It is known in the literature as virtual point splitting due to the intuition that the construction corresponds to splitting the point
p
into
p
and
s
, which is different from the interpretation we presented above.
In any case, the two graphs
\mathrm{G}
and
\tilde{\mathrm{G}}
differ in two ways.
On the one hand, the latter has the extra vertex
s
.
On the other, the half-links
a
and
b
of the former get replaced by the half-links
\tilde{a}
,
\tilde{b}
,
f
, and
e
in the latter.
The vertices and half-links in common between the two graphs will be identified along with the structures supported in them.

Figure 25:
On the left we have a vertex
p
of valency greater than 3 with half-links
a
and
b
attached to it.
We further have the half-links
c=\sigma(a)
and
d=\sigma(b)
, attached respectively to vertices
q
and
r
.
On the right we show a point splitting of said vertex.
A new virtual vertex
\tilde{s}
has been produced, as well as the half-links
e
,
f
,
\tilde{a}
and
\tilde{b}
.

We can prove that the physics on the graphs
\mathrm{G}
and
\tilde{\mathrm{G}}
are equivalent using the techniques we developed in the previous sections.
Namely, consider the paths

R=\{((c,\tilde{a}),(f,e)),((d,\tilde{b}),(f,e))\}\,.

(252)

Then
G=\tilde{G}_{R}
.
The corresponding map
S
is surjective, for each trajectory in
R
has a corresponding link (either
(c,\tilde{a})
or
(d,\tilde{b})
) for which the other trajectory doesn’t go through.
This gives us the map
\iota
obtained by setting

\displaystyle\iota(u)(f,e)=1,\hskip 11.74988pt\iota(u)(c,\tilde{a})=u((c,\tilde{a}),(f,e)),\hskip 11.74988pt\iota(u)(d,\tilde{b})=u((d,\tilde{b}),(f,e))\,,

(253)

for all
u\in\mathcal{E}(\tilde{\mathrm{G}})
.
Thus, all that we are left with checking is that
u\sim\iota(S(u))
, for all
u\in\mathcal{E}(\tilde{\mathrm{G}})
.
This is obtained by noting that
u=g\triangleright\iota(S(u))
, where
g
is equal to the identity for every vertex different than
s
and is equal to
u(f,e)
at
s
.
This is shown in
Fig.

26
.

Figure 26:
On the right we have a field configuration
u\in\mathcal{E}(\tilde{\mathrm{G}})
.
On the left we have
\iota(S(u))
.
The two are equivalent by applying a gauge transformation
u(f,e)
at
s
.

When applying point splitting we are usually in a setting where we understand the dynamics on
G=\tilde{\mathrm{G}}_{R}
.
Accordingly, the Wilson line operators can be immediately translated to operators on
\tilde{\mathrm{G}}
.
Similarly, the electric operators
E(c)
and
E(d)
can be immediately replaced by their counterparts in
\tilde{\mathrm{G}}
.
The counterparts to the operators
E(a)
and
E(b)
are however more delicate.
This issue is not usually addressed in the literature since
E(a)^{2}=E(c)^{2}
on
\mathrm{G}
and thus either can be used to build the Hamiltonian on
\tilde{G}
.
But in our applications we will need to have explicit control over the operator
E(a)
in order to build the Hamiltonian. The way this is done will be discussed in
Sec.

C.4.1
.

Repeated application of this procedure yields graphs that only have trivalent vertices.
With this we can now normalize the states appearing in (
177
) to recover the basis discussed in
[
45
]
.
Given that these states are tensor products of states localized at each site, we need only normalize each of these.
Let us the consider a vertex to which three half-edges
a
,
b
, and
c
are attached.
This situation is shown in
Fig.

11
.
Let us pick as a set of oriented corners
\mathcal{C}=\{(a,b),(b,c),(c,a)\}
.
We then need to compute the vacuum expectation value

\displaystyle C(n(a,b),n(b,c),n(c,a))

(254)

\displaystyle\coloneq\bra{0}\mathcal{L}_{--}^{n(a,b)}(a,b)\mathcal{L}_{--}^{n(b,c)}(b,c)\mathcal{L}_{--}^{n(c,a)}(c,a)\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(a,b)}(a,b)\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(b,c)}(b,c)\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(c,a)}(c,a)\ket{0}\,.

In order to compute this expectation value, let us first study the state

\mathcal{L}_{--}(a,b)\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(a,b)}(a,b)\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(b,c)}(b,c)\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(c,a)}(c,a)\ket{0}\,.

(255)

Since the LSH vacuum is annihilated by all annihilation operators, we can replace the operator above by a commutator.
Then applying the Leibniz rule of derivations, we see that said commutator is the sum of two commutators.
Accordingly, our state of interest is the sum of the states

[\mathcal{L}_{--}(a,b),\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(a,b)}(a,b)]\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(b,c)}(b,c)\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(c,a)}(c,a)\ket{0}\,,

(256)

and

\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(a,b)}(a,b)[\mathcal{L}_{--}(a,b),\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(b,c)}(b,c)\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(c,a)}(c,a)]\ket{0}\,.

(257)

We will now show that the latter of these two vanishes.

Let us recall the commutator (
187
).
With this and the Leibniz rule we conclude that

\displaystyle[\mathcal{L}_{--}(a,b),\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n}(b,c)]=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathcal{L}_{++}^{k-1}(b,c)\mathcal{L}_{-+}(a,c)\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n-k}(b,c)\,.

(258)

But in light of (
186
), all of these terms are actually the same and we obtain the simpler result

[\mathcal{L}_{--}(a,b),\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n}(b,c)]=n\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n-1}(b,c)\mathcal{L}_{-+}(a,c)\,.

(259)

This operator in particular annihilates the vacuum.
Along with further applications of the Leibniz rule and (
185
), one concludes that (
257
) vanishes.

Let us now study the contribution from (
256
).
The Leibniz rule in conjunction with (
187
) and (
195
) shows that

\displaystyle\quantity[\mathcal{L}_{--}(a,b),\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n}(a,b)]=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathcal{L}_{++}^{k-1}(a,b)(N(a)+N(b)+2)\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n-k}(a,b)

(260)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n-1}(a,b)(N(a)+N(b)+2+2(n-k))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n-1}(a,b)(N(a)+N(b)+2+n-1)\,.

Therefore

\displaystyle[\mathcal{L}_{--}(a,b),\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(a,b)}(a,b)]\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(b,c)}(b,c)\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(c,a)}(c,a)

(261)

\displaystyle=n(a,b)\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(a,b)-1}(a,b)\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(b,c)}(b,c)\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(c,a)}(c,a)(N(a)+N(b)+2+n(a,b)-1+n(b,c)+n(c,a))\,.

With this result we can now conclude that

\displaystyle C(n(a,b),n(b,c),n(c,a))=n(a,b)\left(n(a,b)+n(b,c)+n(c,a)+1\right)C(n(a,b)-1,n(b,c),n(c,a))\,.

(262)

This is the fundamental recursion relation from which
C
can be recovered. Repeated applications of it yield

\displaystyle C(n(a,b),n(b,c),n(c,a))=n(a,b)!\frac{(n(a,b)+n(b,c)+n(c,a)+1)!}{(n(b,c)+n(c,a)+1)!}C(0,n(b,c),n(c,a))\,.

(263)

Given that
C
is symmetric under permutations of its entries, and
C(0,0,0)=1
, we conclude that

\displaystyle C(n(a,b),n(b,c),n(c,a))=

(264)

\displaystyle n(a,b)!\frac{(n(a,b)+n(b,c)+n(c,a)+1)!}{(n(b,c)+n(c,a)+1)!}n(b,c)!\frac{(n(b,c)+n(c,a)+1)!}{(n(c,a)+1)!}n(c,a)!(n(c,a)+1)!

\displaystyle=n(a,b)!n(b,c)!n(c,a)!(n(a,b)+n(b,c)+n(c,a)+1)!\,.

Therefore the states

\ket{n}\coloneq\frac{\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(a,b)}(a,b)\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(b,c)}(b,c)\mathcal{L}_{++}^{n(c,a)}(c,a)}{\sqrt{C(n(a,b),n(b,c),n(c,a))}}\ket{0}\,,

(265)

give an orthonormal basis.

As in
[
45
]
, we note that the action of the LSH operators on these states is rather simple.
Indeed, they always amount to a change in
n
and a change in normalization. Observe that

\displaystyle\frac{C(n(a,b)+1,n(b,c),n(c,a))}{C(n(a,b),n(b,c),n(c,a))}=\left(n(a,b)+1)(n(a,b)+n(b,c)+n(c,a)+2\right)\,.

(266)

Therefore, the introduction of the operators

\displaystyle N(a,b)=\frac{1}{2}(N(a)+N(b)-N(c)),\hskip 11.74988ptN(a,b,c)=N(a,b)+N(b,c)+N(c,a)+1\,,

(267)

allows us to express

\mathcal{L}_{++}(a,b)=\Lambda_{+}(a,b)\sqrt{(N(a,b,c)+1)(N(a,b)+1)}

(268)

In here
\Lambda_{+}(a,b)
is the operator that raises and lowers the corresponding quantum number without changing the normalization of the state.
We can further use (
260
) in conjunction with the fact that

\displaystyle\frac{C(n(a,b)-1,n(b,c),n(c,a))}{C(n(a,b),n(b,c),n(c,a))}=\frac{1}{n(a,b)(n(a,b)+n(b,c)+n(c,a)+1)}\,,

(269)

to show that

\mathcal{L}_{--}(a,b)=\Lambda_{-}(a,b)\sqrt{N(a,b)N(a,b,c)}.

(270)

Finally, we can use (
187
) to show that

\mathcal{L}_{-+}(a,b)=\Lambda_{-}(c,a)\Lambda_{+}(b,c)\sqrt{N(c,a)(N(b,c)+1)}\,.

(271)

and therefore

\mathcal{L}_{+-}(a,b)=-\Lambda_{-}(b,c)\Lambda_{+}(c,a)\sqrt{N(b,c)(N(c,a)+1)}\,.

(272)

C.3.2
Maximal tree gauge fixing

In this section we discuss the idea of maximal tree gauge fixing, which identifies a set trajectories in a general graph whose Wilson lines can be set to the identity by choosing appropriate gauge transformations at the vertices.

A tree is a connected graph which has no loops.
Now, consider a graph
\mathrm{G}
that is connected and for which every pair of vertices is connected by a single link at most, i.e. for all
e_{1},e_{2}\in H\times_{\sigma}H
if
s(e_{1})=s(e_{2})
and
t(e_{1})=t(e_{2})
, we then have that
e_{1}=e_{2}
.
We will further demand that there are no closed trajectories of length 1.
A maximal tree on such a graph
\mathrm{G}
is a tree that is a subgraph of
\mathrm{G}
and is maximal, in the sense that the inclusion of any additional link of
\mathrm{G}
would produce a loop.
In the mathematical literature these are more often called spanning trees, for they are equivalently described as trees that have all of the vertices
\mathrm{G}
.
Let us denote the set of half-links of the maximal tree by
M\subseteq H
.
An example is shown in
Fig.

3
.

Now, let us choose a vertex
o\in\mathrm{G}
and an orientation
E_{M}
for all the links that are not in the maximal tree.
For each
e\in E_{M}
define a path
\gamma_{e}
that starts and ends at
o
as follows.
Let
\gamma_{\rightarrow}
be the only path that goes from
o
to
s(e)
along the maximal tree without backtracking (if it wasn’t unique then composing it with the inverse of another one would give a loop along the maximal tree).
Similarly, let
\gamma_{\leftarrow}
be the corresponding path going from
t(e)
to
o
.
Then define
\gamma_{e}=\gamma_{\leftarrow}\cdot e\cdot\gamma_{\rightarrow}
.
Let
R
denote the set of these trajectories.
The resulting
\mathrm{G}_{R}
looks like a flower of loops starting and ending at
o
.
As we will now show, the physics on
\mathrm{G}
and
\mathrm{G}_{R}
are equivalent.

The map
S
is clearly surjective, for
\gamma_{e}
is the only trajectory in
R
going through
e
.
Given a field configuration
u\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G})
, we have

\iota(S(u))(e)=\begin{cases}1&e\in M\times_{\sigma}M\\
u(\gamma_{e})&e\in H\times_{\sigma}H\setminus M\times_{\sigma}M\,.\end{cases}

(273)

We are left with showing that
u\sim\iota(S(u))
.
This is the result of the maximal tree gauge-fixing construction we will describe next.

First let
q
be a vertex in
\mathrm{G}
which is only attached to one link in the maximal tree.
Next, go along the path
\gamma
defined by the maximal tree from
o
to
q
.
At each step
i
, perform the gauge transformation
u(\gamma(i))^{-1}
at
t(\gamma(i))
.
The resulting gauge transformation leaves the identity at
\gamma(i)
and doesn’t disrupt this gauge fixing condition for the previous
\gamma(j)
.
Furthermore, note that after this step the Wilson line at
\gamma(i+1)
is equal to

u(\gamma(i+1))u(\gamma(i))u(\gamma(i-1))\cdots u(\gamma(1))\,.

(274)

Repeating this procedure for all the possible
q
’s shows this gauge fixing procedure.
Every link
e
will pick up a factor of
u(\gamma_{\rightarrow})
on the right for the path that goes through
s(e)
and a factor of
u(\gamma_{\leftarrow})
on the left for the one going through
t(e)
.

C.4
Dynamics

C.4.1
Kogut-Susskind degrees of freedom

So far we have concluded that, as long as every trajectory in
R
has a link that is not traversed by any other trajectory in any direction and every field configuration
u\in\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G})
is gauge equivalent to
\iota(S(u))
, then the theories on
\mathrm{G}
and
\mathrm{G}_{R}
are equivalent at the kinematic level.
In order to make this equivalence useful however, we need to explain how to translate the Hamiltonian of one graph into a Hamiltonian for the other

HS^{*}=S^{*}H_{R}\,.

(275)

In the case of point splitting we will need to translate the dynamical information in
\mathrm{G}_{R}
to a Hamiltonian on
\mathrm{G}
.
In the case of the maximal tree construction we will need to do the opposite.
To the best of our knowledge, there is not a unique construction that works for any coarsening.
We will therefore only answer it in these two cases.
Some general lessons will however be explained as we go along.

Due to the way we have set up the equivalence, it is simplest to understand the relationship between the Wilson line operators on
\mathrm{G}
and on
\mathrm{G}_{R}
.
Namely, we have

\displaystyle\hphantom{{}={}}\matrixelement{u}{S^*U(h_\gamma^{(t)},h_\gamma^{(s)})}{\psi}

\displaystyle=\matrixelement{S(u)}{U(h_\gamma^{(t)},h_\gamma^{(s)})}{\psi}

(276)

\displaystyle=S(u)(h_{\gamma}^{(t)},h_{\gamma}^{(s)})\innerproduct{S(u)}{\psi}

\displaystyle=u(\gamma)\matrixelement{u}{S^*}{\psi}=\matrixelement{u}{U(\gamma)S^*}{\psi}\,,

i.e.

S^{*}U(h_{\gamma}^{(t)},h_{\gamma}^{(s)})=U(\gamma)S^{*}\,.

(277)

This allows us to translate a magnetic Hamiltonian from
\mathrm{G}_{R}
to
\mathrm{G}
immediately.
One simply replaces the Wilson line operators on a link in
\mathrm{G}_{R}
by the Wilson line operator supported on its corresponding path in
\mathrm{G}
.
We have thus solved the problem of the magnetic Hamiltonian under point splitting.
It is however not yet obvious that this solves the problem of the magnetic Hamiltonian under the maximal tree construction.
In order to do this we need to show that in that case the Hamiltonian can be built solely from Wilson lines supported on the trajectories in the corresponding
R
.

In order to do this, we note that every plaquette
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}
in
\mathrm{G}
, a cubic lattice, has a non-empty set
E_{\gamma}\subseteq E_{M}
of links that it goes through that are outside of the maximal tree.
For every
e\in E_{\gamma}
let
\sigma_{e}
be
1
if
\gamma
goes along it with the orientation chosen when constructing the flower or
-1
if it goes against it.
We then have

\tr U(\gamma)=\tr U\quantity(\prod_{e\in E_\gamma} \gamma_e^{\sigma_e})\,,

(278)

where the product is oriented using the ordering in which
\gamma
travels through the links.
The operators on the right hand side are all supported on paths in
R
.
They can therefore be replaced by operators on the corresponding links of
\mathrm{G}_{R}
.
This recovers the solution found in
[
52
]
to the problem of relating the magnetic Hamiltonian of a cubic lattice to that of its corresponding flower.

Let us go back to the general case once more.
Relating the electric operators between
\mathrm{G}
and
\mathrm{G}_{R}
requires us to relate the group actions on the half-links of
\mathrm{G}
with those of
\mathrm{G}_{R}
.
Here the concept of parallel transport arises naturally as follows.
Fix a half-link
h\in H
and a trajectory
\gamma\in T_{n}
(which could potentially be in
R
).
We can then wonder how
\triangleright_{h}
behaves on
\gamma
.
Of course
(g\triangleright_{h}u)(\gamma)=u(\gamma)
if
\gamma
does not go through
h
.
On the other hand, if
\gamma
passes once through
(h,\sigma(h))
we have

(g\triangleright_{h}u)(\gamma)=u(\gamma_{2})gu(h,\sigma(h))u(\gamma_{1})=u(\gamma_{2})gu(\gamma_{2})^{-1}u(\gamma)\,,

(279)

where we have written
\gamma=\gamma_{2}\cdot(h,\sigma(h))\cdot\gamma_{1}
.
This immediately implies that

(u(\gamma_{2})^{-1}gu(\gamma_{2})\triangleright_{h}u)(\gamma)=gu(\gamma)\,.

(280)

This prompts us to the following definition.

Let
h\in H
and
\gamma\in T_{n}
be such that
\pi(h)=t(\gamma)
.
We then define
\circ_{\gamma,h}:G\times\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{G})
by

g\circ_{\gamma,h}u=u(\gamma)gu(\gamma)^{-1}\triangleright_{h}u\,.

(281)

With this definition we have

(g\circ_{\gamma_{2}^{-1},h}u)(\gamma)=gu(\gamma)

(282)

in our previous setting.
In other words, if
\gamma\in R
,

S(g\circ_{\gamma_{2}^{-1},h}u)(h_{\gamma}^{(t)},h_{\gamma}^{(s)})=g\triangleright_{h_{\gamma}^{(t)}}S(u)(h_{\gamma}^{(t)},h_{\gamma}^{(s)})\,.

(283)

Now, assume that the path
\gamma_{2}
is common to all trajectories in
R
which go through
(h,\sigma(h))

Then the generators of
\circ_{\gamma_{2}^{-1},h}
and
\triangleright_{h_{\gamma}^{(t)}}
are related in the quantum theory.
So, let us take some time to explore the general properties of
\circ_{\gamma,h}
.

First of all, the choice of notation reflects that
\circ_{\gamma,h}
need not be a left group action.
Of particular importance to virtual point splitting and the maximal tree construction, is the case in which
\gamma
and
h
are disjoint.
In this case we are guaranteed that
\circ_{\gamma,h}
is, indeed, a left group action.
We can then associate to it an Electric operator.
This mimics the construction of the Electric operators on half-links.
Namely, one first lifts the group action to the quantum level

\displaystyle R_{\gamma,h}(g)\ket{u}\coloneq

\displaystyle\ket{g\circ_{\gamma,h}u}=\ket{u(\gamma)gu(\gamma)^{-1}\triangleright_{h}u}

(284)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{h}(u(\gamma)gu(\gamma)^{-1})\ket{u}\,,

and then defines the electric operator to be its generator

e^{-i\omega E_{X}(\gamma,h)}\coloneq R_{\gamma,h}(e^{\omega X}),\hskip 11.74988ptX\in\mathfrak{g}\,.

(285)

The relationship between the parallel transported operators
E(\gamma,h)
and the operators
E(h)
is clear by noting that

\displaystyle e^{-i\omega E_{X}(\gamma,h)}\ket{u}

\displaystyle=R_{h}(u(\gamma)e^{\omega X}u(\gamma)^{-1})\ket{u}

(286)

\displaystyle=R_{h}(e^{\omega\Ad_{u(\gamma)}X})\ket{u}

\displaystyle=e^{-i\omega E_{\Ad_{u(\gamma)}(X)}(h)}\ket{u}\,,

where
\Ad
denotes the adjoint representation of the structure group
G
on its Lie algebra.
In other words

E_{X}(\gamma,h)\ket{u}=E_{\Ad_{u(\gamma)}(X)}(h)\ket{u}\,.

(287)

On a basis of
\mathfrak{g}
, this becomes

E_{a}(\gamma,h)\ket{u}=\Ad^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[16.70607pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[16.70607pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[15.73682pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.84544pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{u(\gamma)}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}E_{b}(X)(h)\ket{u}\,.

(288)

Multiplying by
X^{a}
, the dual basis of
\mathfrak{g}
under the Killing form, we can further rewrite this in matrix form

E(\gamma,h)\ket{u}=u(\gamma)^{-1}E(h)u(\gamma)\ket{u}\,.

(289)

In here we used the fact that

\displaystyle X^{a}\Ad^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[16.70607pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[16.70607pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[15.73682pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.84544pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{u(\gamma)}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}

\displaystyle=X^{a}\expectationvalue{X^b,\Ad_{u(\gamma)}(X_a)}

(290)

\displaystyle=X^{a}\expectationvalue{\Ad_{u(\gamma)^{-1}}X^b,X_a}

\displaystyle=\Ad_{u(\gamma)^{-1}}(X^{b})

\displaystyle=u(\gamma)^{-1}X^{b}u(\gamma)\,,

due to the
\Ad
-invariance of the Killing form.
Finally, this can be lifted to an operator equation since
\gamma
and
h
do not overlap

E(\gamma,h)=U(\gamma)^{-1}E(h)U(\gamma)\,.

(291)

It is simple to remember the placement of the
U
’s, which guarantee that
E(\gamma,h)
transform under the adjoint representation of the structure group at
s(\gamma)
.

Not only does this expression provide an explicit construction of the parallel transport in terms of other operators that are simpler to implement, but it also guarantees that
E(\gamma,h)^{2}=E(h)^{2}
due to the
Ad
-invariance of the Killing form

K_{ab}=K_{cd}\Ad^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.54858pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.54858pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33041pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.15395pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{g}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}\Ad^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.54858pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.54858pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33041pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.15395pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{g}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.59233pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.59233pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34946pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.18604pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}\,.

(292)

This is also a good place to comment that, another case of general interest is when
\gamma
touches
h
only once at the very end
\gamma(n)_{2}=h
.
This is precisely the case of interest when trying to relate
E(h)
and
E(\sigma(h))
.
As it turns out, in that case instead of a left group action, we will have a right group action.
Thus, these two operators will be related to each other by parallel transport up to a sign.
In this case, since we do have an overlap, we do not have an analogue of (
291
). Interestingly however, it is still true that parallel transport preserves the quadratic Casimir.

Now, we are ready to consider our specific examples.
First, let us treat the simpler example of virtual point splitting.
We have that

S(g\triangleright_{h}u)=g\triangleright_{h}S(u)

(293)

for the half-links
h
in
Fig.

25
which retained their name after point splitting (meaning
c
,
d
and the ones that are outside of the scope of the picture).
This implies that

S^{*}E(e)=E(e)S^{*}\,,

(294)

for those links.
This leaves us with the operator
E(a)
(and, of course, the operator
E(b)
to which the same procedure can be applied) in order to translate the electric information from the original graph
\tilde{G}_{R}
to the one after point splitting
\tilde{G}
.
So, we start by computing

g\triangleright_{a}S(u)=S(g\circ_{(f,e),\tilde{a}}u)\,.

(295)

This is checked by evaluating both sides on every link of
\tilde{G}_{R}
.
In particular, the group action on both sides is trivial on all links except for
(c,a)
, where the equality can be checked explicitely.
We then have

S^{*}E(a)=E((f,e),\tilde{a})S^{*}\,,

(296)

establishing the equivalence of the electric content in the case of point splitting.

Second, let us focus on the maximal tree construction.
For every vertex
p\in\Gamma
, let
\gamma_{p}\in T
be the path starting at the marked vertex
o\in V
and finishing at
p
along the maximal tree.
Using this path we will be able to understand
E(h)^{2}
for all of the half-edges
h\in\pi^{-1}(p)
anchored at
p
except for the ones that
\gamma
goes through.
Fortunately, for every link there is at least one half-link
h
for which
\gamma_{\pi(h)}
does not go through
h
(namely, for each link on the maximal tree there is exactly one, and for each link outside of the maximal tree, both suffice).
Let us then choose such a half-link for every link and define the set of these to be
H_{\text{dyn}}
.
We will refer to these as the set of “dynamical” half-links.
The sum in (
150
) can be performed over this set.

Now, given
h\in H_{\text{dyn}}
, we can consider
E(\gamma_{\pi(h)},h)
, which satisfies

\mathbf{E}(h)^{2}=\mathbf{E}(\gamma_{\pi(h)},h)^{2}.

(297)

The latter however satisfies much simpler properties on
G_{R}
. Indeed, given
\gamma\in R
, there are only four possibilities:

1.

\gamma
and
h
do not overlap.

2.

\gamma
goes along
(h,\sigma(h))
once and doesn’t go through
(\sigma(h),h)
.

3.

\gamma
goes along
(\sigma(h),h)
once and doesn’t go through
(h,\sigma(h))
, and

4.

\gamma
goes both along
(h,\sigma(h))
and
(\sigma(h),h)
, each once.

Even more specially, because of the uniqueness of paths along maximal trees, all
\gamma\in R
that go through
(\sigma(h),h)
share the same trajectory
\gamma_{\pi(h)}
leading up to it.
Let us denote the set of such paths by
R_{h,\leftarrow}
.
Similarly, all
\gamma\in R
that go through
(h,\sigma(h))
share the same trajectory after going through it, namely,
\gamma_{\pi(h)}^{-1}
.
Let us denote the set of such paths by
R_{h,\rightarrow}
.
These remarks lead us to consider

\displaystyle S(g\circ_{\gamma_{\pi(h)},h}u)(h_{\gamma}^{(t)}h_{\gamma}^{(s)})=\begin{cases}S(u)(h_{\gamma}^{(s)}h_{\gamma}^{(t)})&\text{case 1.}\\
(g\triangleright_{h_{\gamma}^{(t)}}S(u))(h_{\gamma}^{(t)}h_{\gamma}^{(s)})&\text{case 2.}\\
(g\triangleright_{h_{\gamma}^{(s)}}S(u))(h_{\gamma}^{(t)}h_{\gamma}^{(s)})&\text{case 3.}\\
(g\triangleright_{h_{\gamma}^{(t)}}\triangleright g\triangleright_{h_{\gamma}^{(s)}}S(u))(h_{\gamma}^{(t)}h_{\gamma}^{(s)})&\text{case 4.}\,,\end{cases}

(298)

by comparison with (
283
).
In other words, the composition of
S
after acting with
\circ_{\gamma_{h},\gamma}
is the same as the composition of
g\triangleright_{h_{\gamma}^{(t)}}
for all
\gamma\in R_{h,\rightarrow}
and
g\triangleright_{h_{\gamma}^{(s)}}
for all
\gamma\in R_{h,\leftarrow}
.
Therefore, we have

E(\gamma_{h},h)S^{*}=S^{*}\quantity(\sum_{\gamma\in R_{h,\leftarrow}}E(h_\gamma^{(s)})+\sum_{\gamma\in R_{h,\leftarrow}}E(h_\gamma^{(t)}))\,.

(299)

In particular, in the case of the maximal tree construction, this gives us a way of translating the quadratic Casimir appearing in the Hamiltonian of a cubic lattice

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}(h)^{2}S^{*}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}(\gamma_{h},h)^{2}S^{*}

(300)

\displaystyle=S^{*}\quantity(\sum_{\gamma\in R_{h,\leftarrow}}\vb{E}(h_\gamma^{(s)})+\sum_{\gamma\in R_{h,\rightarrow}}\vb{E}(h_\gamma^{(t)}))^{2}\,.

This shows how to translate the electric Hamiltonian from that lattice to the flower. In this way we then recover the result shown in
[
42
,
52
]
.

C.4.2
LSH degrees of freedom

In light of equation (
278
), which allows for the transition of the magnetic Hamiltonian from the spatial lattice to the flower, and equation (
277
), which allows for the transition of the magnetic Hamiltonian from flowers to branches, the magnetic Hamiltonian in our formulation will correspond to Wilson loops on branches. The description of these in the LSH formalism has already been discussed in (
208
).

Let us then focus on the electric part. The transition from the spatial lattice to flower, requires (
300
).
Therefore, we need to understand the inner product between two electric operators.
Furthermore, the point splitting process required to transition from flowers to branches, requires us to understand this inner product for electric operators that have been parallel transported back to a common vertex (
296
).

Consider then the inner product

\mathbf{E}(\gamma_{1},h_{1})\cdot\mathbf{E}(\gamma_{2},h_{2})\,

(301)

with
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
two trajectories that start at the same vertex
s(\gamma_{1})=s(\gamma_{2})
and end at
t(\gamma_{1})=\pi(h_{1})
and
t(\gamma_{2})=\pi(h_{2})
.
We will further assume that the two trajectories do not go throught
h_{1}
or
h_{2}
, so that, as operators, the Wilson line and electric operators appearing in this quantity commute.
This geometry is depicted in
Fig.

27
.

Figure 27:
Trajectories
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are depicted in red and blue respectively. They have a common source at the orange vertex
s(\gamma_{1})=s(\gamma_{2})
. A corner trajectory
\gamma_{c}
is also shown in purple. The Wilson line operator on the trajectory
\gamma_{2}\gamma_{1}^{-1}
is equivalent to a the Wilson line operator on a trajectory
\gamma
that first goes through
h_{i}
, then through
\gamma_{c}
, and finally through
h_{f}
. Another corner trajectory
\Gamma_{c}
is shown in brown. This trajectory is obtained by first going through
(h_{1},h_{i})
, then through
\gamma_{c}
, and finally through
(h_{2},h_{f})
. This can equivalently be described as first going through
h_{1}
, then through
\gamma
, and finally through
h_{2}
.

We can therefore manipulate this quantity as one would any other Lie algebra manipulation.
In particular, the invariance of the inner product chosen guarantees that one can shift the parallel transport on
h_{1}
all the way to
h_{2}
.
Thus, defining
\gamma=\gamma_{2}\gamma_{1}^{-1}
, this inner product can be rewritten as

\mathbf{E}(h_{1})\cdot\mathbf{E}(\gamma,h_{2}).

(302)

Observe that
\gamma
then implements the parallel transport of
h_{2}
to
h_{1}
.
In applications it is useful to note that this operator only depends on
U(\gamma)
.
Therefore, if the trajectory
\gamma
backtracks the corresponding Wilson operators will cancel each other.
By eliminating these we can replace the trajectory
\gamma
by a simpler one.
This is the trajectory mentioned in the caption of
Fig.

27
.

On
\mathrm{SU}(2)
with our choice of inner product, we have the orthonormal basis
i\sigma_{i}/2
constructed out of Pauli matrices.
In this basis, we can extract the coefficients of
X=X_{i}i\sigma_{i}/2\in\mathfrak{su}(2)
by

X_{i}=-\tr(i\sigma_i X)\,.

(303)

Then

\displaystyle E(h_{1})_{i}=E_{i\sigma_{i}/2}(h_{1})=-\frac{1}{2}a^{\dagger}(h_{1})\sigma_{i}a(h_{1})=-\frac{1}{2}A_{A+}(h_{1})A^{{{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}(h_{1})\sigma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.03864pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.03864pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13739pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.44695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\,,

(304)

and

\displaystyle E(\gamma,h_{2})_{i}=-\frac{1}{2}A_{A+}(h_{2})A^{{{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}(h_{2})\,\sigma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.0935pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.0935pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.80539pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\quantity(-\tr(i\sigma_i U(\gamma)^{-1}i\sigma_j/2U(\gamma)))\,.

(305)

The trace can explicitly be written in components as

U^{{{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{C}}}(\gamma^{-1})U^{{{D}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.73022pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.59392pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}}}(\gamma)\sigma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.03864pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.03864pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13739pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.44695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\sigma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.0935pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.0935pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.80539pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.73022pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.59392pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{D}}}\,.

(306)

Thus the inner product is the contraction of the tensor

A_{A+}(h_{1})A^{{{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}(h_{1})A_{C+}(h_{2})A^{{{D}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.73022pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.59392pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}(h_{2})U^{{{E}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.88777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.88777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.12097pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.28416pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.01175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.01175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.22305pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.34248pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{F}}}(\gamma^{-1})U^{{{G}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97862pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97862pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.96623pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83247pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25656pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{H}}}(\gamma)\,,

(307)

with

\frac{1}{8}\sigma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.03864pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.03864pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13739pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.44695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}\sigma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.0935pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.0935pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.80539pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.73022pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.59392pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{D}}}\sigma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.03864pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.03864pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13739pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.44695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{H}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.01175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.01175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.22305pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.34248pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97862pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97862pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.96623pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83247pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{E}}}\sigma^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.0935pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.0935pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.80539pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{F}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25656pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{j}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.88777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.88777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.12097pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.28416pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{G}}}=\frac{1}{2}\delta^{A}_{E}\delta^{H}_{B}\delta^{C}_{G}\delta^{F}_{D}-\frac{1}{4}\delta^{A}_{B}\delta^{H}_{E}\delta^{C}_{G}\delta^{F}_{D}-\frac{1}{4}\delta^{A}_{E}\delta^{H}_{B}\delta^{C}_{D}\delta^{F}_{G}+\frac{1}{8}\delta^{A}_{B}\delta^{H}_{E}\delta^{C}_{D}\delta^{F}_{G}\,,

(308)

where the expression in terms of Kronecker deltas can be obtained from the Fierz identity of the Pauli matrices.

For example, the fact that
t(\gamma)=s(\gamma^{-1})=\pi(h_{2})
implies that the index
G
can only be contracted with the
C
or the
F
index.
This suggests a reordering of the product of operators into

A_{A+}(h_{1})U^{{{E}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.88777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.88777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.12097pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.28416pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.01175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.01175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.22305pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.34248pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{F}}}(\gamma^{-1})A^{{{D}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.73022pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.59392pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}(h_{2})A_{C+}(h_{2})U^{{{G}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97862pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97862pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.96623pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83247pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25656pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{H}}}(\gamma)A^{{{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}(h_{1})\,.

(309)

In doing so we however switched the order of the
h_{2}
factors, so that we have to include the additional operator

-\delta^{D}_{C}A_{A+}(h_{1})U^{{{E}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.88777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.88777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.12097pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.28416pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.01175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.01175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.22305pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.34248pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{F}}}(\gamma^{-1})U^{{{G}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.97862pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.97862pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.96623pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.83247pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25656pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{H}}}(\gamma)A^{{{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}(h_{1})\,.

(310)

This additional operator is however simpler to study, since gauge invariance demands that
G
and
F
be contracted.
But then the Wilson lines are multiplied and lead to a
\delta^{E}_{F}
.
Therefore the four possible contractions must be proportional to
-N(h_{1})
.

Let us now make these contractions explicit.
The term coming from (
310
) is

-\quantity(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{2}{4}-\frac{2}{4}+\frac{4}{8})N(h_{1})=0\,,

(311)

where the 4 terms in the bracket correspond to the 4 possible combinations of Kronecker deltas in
Eq.

308
, such that this term vanishes.
Now we can perform the contractions of the non-zero contribution (
309
).
The last three combinations of Kronecker deltas include contractions of the Wilson lines.
Thus, they are
\gamma
independent.
Therefore gauge invariance demands that they are functions of
N(h_{1})
and
N(h_{2})
.
In fact, because of the order that we wrote the operators in, these contractions are proportional to
N(h_{1})(N(h_{2})+2)
.
Performing these contractions explicitly, we have

\displaystyle\quantity(-\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{4}+\frac{2}{8})N(h_{1})(N(h_{2})+2)=-\frac{1}{4}N(h_{1})(N(h_{2})+2)\,.

(312)

We are then left with the first combination of Kronecker deltas, which gives

\frac{1}{2}A_{A+}(h_{1})U^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.73022pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.59392pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{D}}}(\gamma^{-1})A^{{{D}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.56999pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.73022pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.59392pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}(h_{2})A_{C+}(h_{2})U^{{{C}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.95457pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.25655pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.30225pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}(\gamma)A^{{{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}(h_{1})\,.

(313)

This is a product of two terms that can be written in the LSH formulation. To see this, note that

\displaystyle A_{A+}(h_{2})U^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}(\gamma)A^{{{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}(h_{1})=A_{A+}(h_{2})U^{{{A}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.68509pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.09242pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.15pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}(h_{f})t^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.74062pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.55363pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}(\gamma(n))U^{{{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.81798pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.72401pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.80249pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{c}}}(\gamma_{c})t^{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.59233pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.59233pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.34946pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.18604pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.8656pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81862pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81757pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}(\gamma(1))U^{{{\dagger d}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.54239pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.54239pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[8.2695pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0527pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{B}}}(h_{i})A^{{{B}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.97504pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7767pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.4pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.1459pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.37212pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.39645pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{-}}}(h_{1})\,.

(314)

Using (
195
) one can write
